On the other hand, evaluation in mode
\mathsf{priv}
can be safely performed; indeed, resource
p
can be used in mode
\mathsf{priv}
since
\mathsf{priv}\preceq\mathsf{pub}
, as shown in the second proof tree in
Fig.

3
.

Formal definition of resource-aware semantics

As anticipated, rules defining the instrumented semantics are given on a fine-grained version of the language.
This long-standing approach
(
LevyPT03
)
is used to clearly separate effect-free from effectful expressions (
computations
), and to make the evaluation strategy, relevant for the latter, explicit through the sequencing construct (
let-in
), rather than fixed a-priori. In our calculus, the computational effect is
divergence
, so the effectful expressions are possibily diverging, whereas those effect-free will be called
value expressions
4
4

4

They are often called just “values” in literature, though, as already noted in
(
LevyPT03
)
, they are not values in the operational sense, that is, results of the evaluation; here we keep the two notions distinct. Values turn out to be value expressions with no free variables, except
those under a lambda.
. Note that, as customary, expressions are defined on top of value expressions, whereas the converse does not hold; this stratification makes the technical development modular, as will be detailed in the following.

The fine-grained syntax is shown in
Fig.

4
.

\begin{array}[]{lcll}\mathit{v}&::=&\mathit{x}\mid\texttt{rec}\,\mathit{f}.\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}\mid\texttt{unit}\mid\langle^{r}\mathit{v}_{1},\mathit{v}_{2}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{s}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-6.29688pt{s}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{s}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-6.29688pt{s}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{s}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-5.64064pt{s}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{s}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-5.64064pt{s}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}\mid\texttt{inl}^{r}\mathit{v}\mid\texttt{inr}^{r}\mathit{v}&\mbox{value expression}\\
\mathit{e}&::=&\texttt{return}\ \mathit{v}\mid\texttt{let}\ \mathit{x}=\mathit{e}_{1}\ \texttt{in}\ \mathit{e}_{2}\mid\mathit{v}_{1}\,{\!\mathit{v}_{2}}&\mbox{expression}\\
&\mid&\texttt{match}\ \mathit{v}\ \texttt{with}\ \texttt{unit}\ \rightarrow\mathit{e}&\mbox{}\\
&\mid&\texttt{match}\ \mathit{v}\ \texttt{with}\ \langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle\rightarrow\mathit{e}&\mbox{}\\
&\mid&\texttt{match}\ \mathit{v}\ \texttt{with}\ \texttt{inl}\,\mathit{x}_{1}\rightarrow\mathit{e}_{1}\ \texttt{or}\ \texttt{inr}\,\mathit{x}_{2}\rightarrow\mathit{e}_{2}&\mbox{}\end{array}

Figure 4:
Fine-grained syntax

As said above, there are two distinct syntactic categories of value expressions and expressions.
For simplicity we use the same metavariable
\mathit{e}
of the surface syntax for the latter, though the defining production is changed. This is justified by the well-known fact that expressions of the surface language can be encoded in the fine-grained syntax, by using the
let-in
construct, and the injection of value expressions into expressions made explicit by the
return
keyword.

The resource-aware semantics is formally defined in
Fig.

5
. Corresponding to the two syntactic categories, the semantics is expressed by two distinct judgments,
\mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho^{\prime}
in the top section, and
\mathit{e}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho^{\prime}
in the bottom section, with the latter defined on top of the former. Hence, the metarules in
Fig.

5
can be equivalently seen as

•

a unique inference system defining the union of the two judgments

•

an inference system in the top section, defining
\mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho^{\prime}
, and an inference system in the bottom section, defining
\mathit{e}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho^{\prime}
, where the previous judgment acts as a side condition.

For simplicity, we use the same notation for the two judgments, and in the bottom section of
Fig.

5
we write both judgments as premises, taking the first view. However, the second view will be useful later to allow a modular technical development.

\begin{array}[]{l}\scriptstyle{\textsc{(var)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{\mathit{x}\mid\rho,\mathit{x}:\langle{s},{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho,\mathit{x}:\langle{s^{\prime}},{\mathbf{v}}\rangle}\begin{array}[]{l}r+s^{\prime}\preceq s\end{array}\qquad\scriptstyle{\textsc{(fun)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{\texttt{rec}\,\mathit{f}.\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r}\texttt{rec}\,\mathit{f}.\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}\mid\rho}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[25.83325pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(unit)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{\texttt{unit}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r}\texttt{unit}\mid\rho}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\qquad\scriptstyle{\textsc{(pair)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathit{v}_{1}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r{\cdot}r_{1}}\mathbf{v}_{1}\mid\rho_{1}\quad\mathit{v}_{2}\mid\rho_{1}\Rightarrow_{r{\cdot}r_{2}}\mathbf{v}_{2}\mid\rho_{2}}{\langle^{r_{1}}\mathit{v}_{1},\mathit{v}_{2}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r}\langle^{r_{1}}\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}\mid\rho_{2}}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[17.22217pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(inl)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r{\cdot}s}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho^{\prime}}{\texttt{inl}^{s}\mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r}\texttt{inl}^{s}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho^{\prime}}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\qquad\scriptstyle{\textsc{(inl)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r{\cdot}s}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho^{\prime}}{\texttt{inr}^{s}\mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r}\texttt{inr}^{s}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho^{\prime}}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[17.22217pt]
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{l}\\
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(ret)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{s}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho^{\prime}}{\texttt{return}\ \mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho^{\prime}}\begin{array}[]{l}r\preceq s\neq\mathbf{0}\end{array}\qquad\scriptstyle{\textsc{(let)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{e}_{1}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{s}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho^{\prime\prime}\\
\mathit{e}_{2}[\mathit{x}^{\prime}/\mathit{x}]\mid\rho^{\prime\prime},\mathit{x}^{\prime}:\langle{s},{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\mid\rho^{\prime}\end{array}}{\texttt{let}\ \mathit{x}=\mathit{e}_{1}\ \texttt{in}\ \mathit{e}_{2}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\mid\rho^{\prime}}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{x}^{\prime}\ \mbox{fresh}\end{array}\\[17.22217pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(app)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{v}_{1}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{s}\texttt{rec}\,\mathit{f}.\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}\mid\rho_{1}\quad\mathit{v}_{2}\mid\rho_{1}\Rightarrow_{t}\mathbf{v}_{2}\mid\rho_{2}\\
\mathit{e}[\mathit{f}^{\prime}/\mathit{f}][\mathit{x}^{\prime}/\mathit{x}]\mid\rho_{2},\mathit{f}^{\prime}:\langle{s_{2}},{\texttt{rec}\,\mathit{f}.\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}}\rangle,\mathit{x}^{\prime}:\langle{t},{\mathbf{v}_{2}}\rangle\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho^{\prime}\end{array}}{\mathit{v}_{1}\,{\!\mathit{v}_{2}}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho^{\prime}}\begin{array}[]{l}s_{1}+s_{2}\preceq s\\
s_{1}\neq\mathbf{0}\\
\mathit{f}^{\prime},\mathit{x}^{\prime}\ \mbox{fresh}\end{array}\\[21.52771pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(match-u)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{s}\texttt{unit}\mid\rho^{\prime\prime}\quad\mathit{e}\mid\rho^{\prime\prime}\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho^{\prime}}{\texttt{match}\ \mathit{v}\ \texttt{with}\ \texttt{unit}\ \rightarrow\mathit{e}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho}\begin{array}[]{l}s\neq\mathbf{0}\end{array}\\[21.52771pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(match-p)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{s}\langle^{r_{1}}\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}\mid\rho^{\prime\prime}\\
\mathit{e}[\mathit{x}^{\prime}/\mathit{x}][\mathit{y}^{\prime}/\mathit{y}]\mid\rho^{\prime\prime},\mathit{x}^{\prime}:\langle{s{\cdot}r_{1}},{\mathbf{v}_{1}}\rangle,\mathit{y}^{\prime}:\langle{s{\cdot}r_{2}},{\mathbf{v}_{2}}\rangle\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho^{\prime}\end{array}}{\texttt{match}\ \mathit{v}\ \texttt{with}\ \langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle\rightarrow\mathit{e}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho^{\prime}}\begin{array}[]{l}s\neq\mathbf{0}\\
\mathit{x}^{\prime},\mathit{y}^{\prime}\ \mbox{fresh}\end{array}\\[21.52771pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(match-l)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{t}\texttt{inl}^{s}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho^{\prime\prime}\\
\mathit{e}_{1}[\mathit{y}/\mathit{x}_{1}]\mid\rho^{\prime\prime},\mathit{y}:\langle{t{\cdot}s},{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\mid\rho^{\prime}\end{array}}{\texttt{match}\ \mathit{v}\ \texttt{with}\ \texttt{inl}\,\mathit{x}_{1}\rightarrow\mathit{e}_{1}\ \texttt{or}\ \texttt{inr}\,\mathit{x}_{2}\rightarrow\mathit{e}_{2}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\mid\rho^{\prime}}\begin{array}[]{l}t\neq\mathbf{0}\\
\mathit{y}\ \mbox{fresh}\end{array}\\[21.52771pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(match-r)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{t}\texttt{inr}^{s}\mathbf{v}\mid\rho^{\prime\prime}\\
\mathit{e}_{2}[\mathit{y}/\mathit{x}_{1}]\mid\rho^{\prime\prime},\mathit{y}:\langle{t{\cdot}s},{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\mid\rho^{\prime}\end{array}}{\texttt{match}\ \mathit{v}\ \texttt{with}\ \texttt{inl}\,\mathit{x}_{1}\rightarrow\mathit{e}_{1}\ \texttt{or}\ \texttt{inr}\,\mathit{x}_{2}\rightarrow\mathit{e}_{2}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\mid\rho^{\prime}}\begin{array}[]{l}t\neq\mathbf{0}\\
\mathit{y}\ \mbox{fresh}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 5:
Resource-aware semantics

Rules for value expressions just replace variables by values; the reduction cannot diverge, but is resource-consuming, hence it can get stuck.

In particular, in
(var)
, which is the key rule where resources are consumed,
a variable is replaced by its associated value,
and its grade
s
decreases to
s^{\prime}
, burning an amount
r^{\prime}
of resource which has to be at least the reduction grade.
The side condition
r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}\preceq s
ensures that the initial grade allows to consume the
r^{\prime}
amount, leaving a residual grade
s^{\prime}
.
Note that the consumed amount is
not
required to be exactly
r
, that is, there is no constraint that the semantics should not “waste” resources. Hence, reduction is largely non-deterministic; it will be the responsibility of the type system to ensure that there is at least one reduction that does not get stuck.

The other rules for value expressions propagate rule
(var)
to subterms which are variables. In rules for “data containers”
(pair)
,
(in-l)
, and
(in-r)
, the components are evaluated with the evaluation grade of the compound value expression, multiplied by that of the component.

Whereas evaluation of value expressions may have grade
\mathbf{0}
, when they are actually
used
, that is, are subterms of expressions, they should be evaluated with a non-zero grade, as required by a side condition in the corresponding rules in the bottom section of
Fig.

5
.

In rule
(ret)
, the evaluation grade of the value expression should be enough to cover the current evaluation grade.
In rule
(let)
, expressions
\mathit{e}_{1}
and
\mathit{e}_{2}
are evaluated sequentially, the latter in an environment where the local variable
\mathit{x}
has been added as available resource, modulo renaming with a fresh variable to avoid clashes, with the value and grade obtained by the evaluation of
\mathit{e}_{1}
.

In rule
(app)
, an application
\mathit{v}_{1}\,{\!\mathit{v}_{2}}
is evaluated by first consuming the resources needed to obtain a value from
\mathit{v}_{1}
and
\mathit{v}_{2}
, with the former expected to be a (possibly recursive) function. Then, the function body is evaluated in an environment where the function name and the parameter have been added as available resources, modulo renaming with fresh variables.
The function should be produced in a “number of copies”, that is, with a grade
s
, enough to cover both all the future recursive calls (
s_{2}
) and the current use (
s_{1}
); in particular, for a non-recursive call,
s_{2}
could be
\mathbf{0}
. Instead, the current use should be non-zero since we are actually using the function.

Note that
s_{1}
is arbitrary, and could not be replaced by a sound default grade: notably,
\mathbf{1}
would not work for grade algebras where there are grades between
\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{1}
, as happens, e.g.,
for privacy levels.

Note also that, in this rule as in others, there is no required relation between the reduction grades of some premises (in this case,
s
and
t
) and that of the consequence, here
r
.
Of course, depending on the choice of such grades, reduction could either proceed or get stuck due to resource exhaustion; the role of the type system is exactly to show that there is a choice which prevents the latter case.

Rules for match constructs, namely
(match-u)
,
(match-p)
,
(match-l)
, and
(match-r)
, all follow the same pattern. The resources needed to obtain a value from the value expression to be matched are consumed, and then the continuation is evaluated. In rule
(match-u)
, there is no value-passing from the matching expression to the continuation, hence their evaluation grades are independent. In rule
(match-p)
, instead, the continuation is evaluated in an environment where the two variables in the pattern have been added as available resources, again modulo renaming. The values associated to the two variables are the ones of the corresponding component of the expression to be matched, whereas the grades are the evaluation grade of the expression, multiplied by the grade of the component. Rules
(match-l)
and
(match-r)
are analogous.

Besides the standard typing errors, an evaluation graded
r
can get stuck (formally, no judgment can be derived) when rule
(var)
cannot be applied since its side condition does not hold. Informally, some resource (variable) is exhausted, that is, can no longer be replaced by its value.
Also note that the instrumented reduction is non-deterministic, due to rule
(var)
. That is, when a resource is needed, it can be consumed in different ways; hence, soundness of the type system will be
soundness-may
, meaning that
there exists
a computation which does not get stuck.

We end this section by illustrating resource consumption in a non-terminating computation.

Example 3.3
.

Consider the function
\mathsf{div}=\texttt{rec}\,\mathit{f}.\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{y}\texttt{;}\mathit{f}\,{\!\mathit{x}}
, which clearly diverges on any argument, by using infinitely many times the external resource
\mathit{y}
. In
Fig.

6
we show the (incomplete) proof tree for the evaluation of an application
\mathit{y}\texttt{;}\mathit{f}\,{\!\mathit{x}}
, in an environment where
\mathit{f}
denotes the function
\mathsf{div}
, and
\mathit{y}
and
\mathit{x}
denote
unit
. For simplicity, as in previous examples, we omit
\mathbf{1}
grades, and renaming of variables; moreover, we abbreviate by
\langle{r}{,}{s}{,}{t}\rangle
the environment
\mathit{y}:\langle{r},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle,\mathit{f}:\langle{s},{\mathsf{div}}\rangle,\mathit{x}:\langle{t},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle
.

\begin{array}[]{l}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\hskip 4.25006pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 44.37361pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.46504pt,depth=-1.78502pt,width=88.74722pt\hbox{\kern 2.55005pt$$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{y}\mid\langle{r_{0}}{,}{s_{0}}{,}{\mathbf{1}}\rangle{\Rightarrow}\texttt{u}\mid\langle{r_{1}}{,}{s_{0}}{,}{\mathbf{1}}\rangle$}}}}\hskip 4.25006pt\penalty\hskip 4.25006pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\hskip 4.25006pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 48.35278pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.46504pt,depth=-1.78502pt,width=96.70557pt\hbox{\kern 2.55005pt$$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{f}\mid\langle{r_{1}}{,}{s_{0}}{,}{\mathbf{1}}\rangle{\Rightarrow_{s_{0}}}\mathsf{div}\mid\langle{r_{1}}{,}{\mathbf{0}}{,}{\mathbf{1}}\rangle$}}}}\hskip 4.25006pt\penalty\!\!\!\hskip 4.25006pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 40.9263pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.46504pt,depth=-1.78502pt,width=81.8526pt\hbox{\kern 2.55005pt$$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{x}\mid\langle{r_{1}}{,}{\mathbf{0}}{,}{\mathbf{1}}\rangle{\Rightarrow}\texttt{u}\mid\langle{r_{1}}{,}{\mathbf{0}}{,}{\mathbf{0}}\rangle$}}}}\hskip 4.25006pt\penalty\!\!\!\hskip 4.25006pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 27.96954pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\ldots$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.46504pt,depth=-1.78502pt,width=63.43909pt\hbox{\kern 2.55005pt$$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{y}\texttt{;}\mathit{f}\,{\!\mathit{x}}\mid\langle{r_{1}}{,}{s_{1}}{,}{\mathbf{1}}\rangle{\Rightarrow}?$}}}}\hskip 4.25006pt\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.46504pt,depth=-1.78502pt,width=259.49768pt\hbox{\kern 2.55005pt$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(app)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 102.23906pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{f}\,{\!\mathit{x}}\mid\langle{r_{1}}{,}{s_{0}}{,}{\mathbf{1}}\rangle{\Rightarrow}?$}}}}\hskip 4.25006pt\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.46504pt,depth=-1.78502pt,width=383.52318pt\hbox{\kern 2.55005pt$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(match-u)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 160.04204pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{y}\texttt{;}\mathit{f}\,{\!\mathit{x}}\mid\langle{r_{0}}{,}{s_{0}}{,}{\mathbf{1}}\rangle{\Rightarrow}?$}}}}\end{array}

Figure 6:
Example of resource-aware evaluation: consumption/divergency

In this way, we can focus on the key feature that the (tentative) proof tree shows: the body of
\mathsf{div}
is evaluated infinitely many times, in a sequence of environments, starting from the root, where the grades of the external resource
\mathit{y}
, assuming that each time it is consumed by
\mathbf{1}
, are as follows:

r_{0}=r_{1}+\mathbf{1}

r_{1}=r_{2}+\mathbf{1}
…
r_{k}=r_{k+1}+\mathbf{1}
…

In the case of the resource
\mathit{f}
, at each recursive call, the function must be produced with a grade which is the sum of its current usage (assumed again to be
\mathbf{1}
) and the grade which will be associated to (a fresh copy of)
\mathit{f}
in the environment, to evaluate the body. As a consequence, we also get:

s_{0}=s_{1}+\mathbf{1}

s_{1}=s_{2}+\mathbf{1}
…
s_{k}=s_{k+1}+\mathbf{1}
…

Let us now see what happens depending on the underlying grade algebra, considering
\mathit{y}
(an analogous reasoning applies to
\mathit{f}
).
Taking the grade algebra of natural numbers of
Example

2.3
(
1
), it is easy to see that the above sequence of constraints can be equivalently expressed as:

r_{1}=r_{0}-1

r_{2}=r_{1}-1
…
r_{k+1}=r_{k}-1
…

Thus, in a finite number of steps, the grade of
\mathit{y}
in the environment becomes
0
, hence the proof tree cannot be continued since we can no longer extract the associated value by rule
(var)
.
In other words, the computation is stuck due to resource consumption.

Assume now to take, instead, natural numbers extended with
\infty
, as defined in
Example

2.3
(
7
).
In this case, if we start with
r_{0}=\infty
, intuitively meaning that
\mathit{y}
can be used infinitely many times, evaluation can proceed forever by taking
r_{k}=\infty
for all
k
. The same happens if we take a non-quantitative grade algebra, e.g., that of privacy levels; we can have
r_{k}=\mathsf{public}
for all
k
. However, interpreting the rules in
Fig.

5
in the standard inductive way,
the semantics we get
does not
formalize such non-terminating evaluation, since we only consider judgments with a
finite
proof tree. We will see in
Section

7
how to extend the semantics to model
non-terminating computations as well.

4
Resource-aware type system

Types, defined in
Fig.

7
, are those expected for the constructs in the syntax: functional,
Unit
, (tensor) product, sum, and (equi-)recursive types, obtained by interpreting the productions
coinductively
, so that infinite
5
5

5

More precisely,
regular
terms, that is, those with finitely many distinct subterms.
terms are allowed. However, they are
graded
, that is, decorated with a grade, and the type subterms are graded. Moreover, accordingly with the fact that functions are possibly recursive, arrows in functional types are decorated with a grade as well, called
recursion grade
in the following, expressing the recursive usage of the function; thus, functional types decorated with
\mathbf{0}
are non-recursive.

\begin{array}[]{lcll}\tau,\sigma&::=&\mathit{T}\rightarrow_{s}\mathit{S}\mid\texttt{Unit}\mid\mathit{T}\otimes\mathit{S}\mid\mathit{T}+\mathit{S}&\mbox{non-graded type}\\
\mathit{T},\mathit{S}&::=&{\tau}^{r}&\mbox{graded type}\\
\gamma&::=&\mathit{x}_{1}:r_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}:r_{n}&\mbox{grade context}\\
\Gamma,\Delta&::=&\mathit{x}_{1}:_{r_{1}}\tau_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}:_{r_{n}}\tau_{n}&\mbox{(type-and-grade) context}\end{array}

Figure 7:
Types and (type-and-grade) contexts

In (type-and-grade) contexts, also defined in
Fig.

7
, order is immaterial and
\mathit{x}_{i}\neq\mathit{x}_{j}
for
i\neq j
; hence, they represent maps from variables to pairs consisting of a grade, sometimes called
coeffect

(
PetricekOM13
;
PetricekOM14
;
BrunelGMZ14
)
when used in this position, and a (non-graded) type. We write
\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
for
\{\mathit{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}\}
. Equivalently, a type-and-grade context can be seen as a pair consisting of the standard type context
\mathit{x}_{1}:\tau_{1}\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}:\tau_{n}
, and the grade context
\mathit{x}_{1}:r_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}:r_{n}
, the latter representing a function
\gamma
from variables into grades with finite support, that is,
\gamma(\mathit{x})\neq\mathbf{0}
for finitely many
\mathit{x}\in\mathsf{V}
.

We define the following operations on contexts:

•

a partial order
\preceq

\displaystyle\emptyset

\displaystyle\preceq\emptyset

\displaystyle\mathit{x}:_{s}\tau,\Gamma

\displaystyle\preceq\mathit{x}:_{r}\tau,\Delta

if
s\preceq r
and
\Gamma\preceq\Delta

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle\preceq\mathit{x}:_{r}\tau,\Delta

if
\mathit{x}\not\in\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
and
\Gamma\preceq\Delta
and
\mathbf{0}\preceq r

•

a sum
+

\displaystyle\emptyset+\Gamma

\displaystyle=\Gamma

\displaystyle(\mathit{x}:_{s}\tau,\Gamma)+(\mathit{x}:_{r}\tau,\Delta)

\displaystyle=\mathit{x}:_{s+r}\tau,(\Gamma+\Delta)

\displaystyle(\mathit{x}:_{s}\tau,\Gamma)+\Delta

\displaystyle=\mathit{x}:_{s}\tau,(\Gamma+\Delta)

if
\mathit{x}\notin\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)

•

a scalar multiplication
{\cdot}

\displaystyle s{\cdot}\emptyset=\emptyset

\displaystyle s{\cdot}(\mathit{x}:_{r}\tau,\Gamma)=\mathit{x}:_{s{\cdot}r}\tau,(s{{\cdot}}\Gamma)

These operations on type-and-grade contexts are obtained by lifting the corresponding operations
on grade contexts, which are the pointwise extension of those on grades,
to handle types.
In this step, the addition becomes partial since a variable in the domain of both contexts is required to have the same type.

Remark 4.1
.

It is important to note that, when overapproximating contexts with the partial order, new resources can be added only with a discardable grade. For instance, in the linearity grade algebra of
Example

2.3
(
2
),
\Gamma\not\preceq(\Gamma,\mathit{y}:_{1}\sigma)
. In other words, the partial order models
no-waste
approximation.

In
Fig.

8
, we give the typing rules, which are
parameterized
on the underlying grade algebra.
As for instrumented reduction, the resource-aware type system is formalized by two judgments,
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}:\mathit{T}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}
, for value expressions and expressions, respectively. However, differently from reduction, the two judgments are mutually recursive, due to rule
(t-fun)
, hence the metarules in
Fig.

8
define a unique judgment which is their union.
We only comment the most significant points.

\begin{array}[]{l}\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-sub-v)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}:{\tau}^{r}}{\Delta\vdash\mathit{v}:{\tau}^{s}}\begin{array}[]{l}s\preceq r\\
\Gamma\preceq\Delta\end{array}\qquad\par\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-var)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{\mathit{x}:_{r}\tau\vdash\mathit{x}:{\tau}^{r}}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[17.22217pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-fun)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma,\mathit{f}:_{s}{\tau_{1}}^{r_{1}}\rightarrow_{s}{\tau_{2}}^{r_{2}},\mathit{x}:_{r_{1}}\tau_{1}\vdash\mathit{e}:{\tau_{2}}^{r_{2}}}{r{\cdot}\Gamma\vdash\texttt{rec}\,\mathit{f}.\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}:{({\tau_{1}}^{r_{1}}\rightarrow_{s}{\tau_{2}}^{r_{2}})}^{r}}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\qquad\par\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-unit)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{\emptyset\vdash\texttt{unit}:{\texttt{Unit}}^{r}}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\par\\[17.22217pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-pair)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma_{1}\vdash\mathit{v}_{1}:\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}\quad\Gamma_{2}\vdash\mathit{v}_{2}:\tau_{2}^{r_{2}}}{r{\cdot}(\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2})\vdash\langle^{r_{1}}\mathit{v}_{1},\mathit{v}_{2}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}:{(\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}\otimes\tau_{2}^{r_{2}})}^{r}}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[17.22217pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-inl)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}:\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}}{r{\cdot}\Gamma\vdash\texttt{inl}^{r_{1}}\mathit{v}:{(\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}+\tau_{2}^{r_{2}})}^{r}}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\qquad\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-inr)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}:\tau_{2}^{r_{2}}}{r{\cdot}\Gamma\vdash\texttt{inr}^{r_{2}}\mathit{v}:{(\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}+\tau_{2}^{r_{2}})}^{r}}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[17.22217pt]
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{l}\\
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-sub)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:{\tau}^{r}}{\Delta\vdash\mathit{e}:{\tau}^{s}}\begin{array}[]{l}s\preceq r\\
\Gamma\preceq\Delta\end{array}\quad\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-ret)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}:{\tau}^{r}}{\Gamma\vdash\texttt{return}\ \mathit{v}:{\tau}^{r}}\begin{array}[]{l}r\neq\mathbf{0}\end{array}\\[17.22217pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-let)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma_{1}\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}:{\tau_{1}}^{r_{1}}\ \ \Gamma_{2},\mathit{x}:_{r_{1}}\tau_{1}\vdash\mathit{e}_{2}:{\tau_{2}}^{r_{2}}}{\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\vdash\texttt{let}\ \mathit{x}=\mathit{e}_{1}\ \texttt{in}\ \mathit{e}_{2}:{\tau_{2}}^{r_{2}}}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[17.22217pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-app)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma_{1}\vdash\mathit{v}_{1}:{({\tau_{1}}^{r_{1}}\rightarrow_{s}{\tau_{2}}^{r_{2}})}^{(r+r{\cdot}s)}\ \Gamma_{2}\vdash\mathit{v}_{2}:{\tau_{1}}^{r{\cdot}r_{1}}}{\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\vdash\mathit{v}_{1}\,{\!\mathit{v}_{2}}:{\tau_{2}}^{r{\cdot}r_{2}}}\begin{array}[]{l}r\neq\mathbf{0}\end{array}\\[17.22217pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-match-u)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma_{1}\vdash\mathit{v}:{\texttt{Unit}}^{r}\quad\Gamma_{2}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}}{\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\vdash\texttt{match}\ \mathit{v}\ \texttt{with}\ \texttt{unit}\ \rightarrow\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}}\begin{array}[]{l}r\neq\mathbf{0}\end{array}\\[17.22217pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-match-p)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma_{1}\vdash\mathit{v}:{(\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}\otimes\tau_{2}^{r_{2}})}^{r}\quad\Gamma_{2},\mathit{x}:_{r{\cdot}r_{1}}\tau,\mathit{y}:_{r{\cdot}r_{2}}\tau_{2}\vdash\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}}{\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\vdash\texttt{match}\ \mathit{v}\ \texttt{with}\ \langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle\rightarrow\mathit{e}:\mathit{T}}\begin{array}[]{l}r\neq\mathbf{0}\end{array}\\[17.22217pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-match-in)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma_{1}\vdash\mathit{v}:{(\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}+\tau_{2}^{r_{2}})}^{r}\quad\Gamma_{2},\mathit{x}:_{r{\cdot}r_{1}}\tau_{1}\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}:\mathit{T}\quad\Gamma_{2},\mathit{x}:_{r{\cdot}r_{2}}\tau_{2}\vdash\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}}{\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\vdash\texttt{match}\ \mathit{v}\ \texttt{with}\ \texttt{inl}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathit{e}_{1}\ \texttt{or}\ \texttt{inr}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathit{e}_{2}:\mathit{T}}\begin{array}[]{l}r\neq\mathbf{0}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 8:
Typing rules

In rule
(t-sub-v)
and
(t-sub)
, the context can be made more general, and the grade of the type more specific. This means that, on one hand, variables can get less constraining grades. For instance, assuming affinity grades as in
Example

2.3
(
2
), an expression which can be typechecked assuming to use a given variable at most once (grade 1) can be typechecked with no constraints (grade
\omega
). On the other hand, an expression can get a more constraining grade. For instance, an expression of grade
\omega
can be used where a grade 1 is required.

If we take
r=\mathbf{1}
, then rule
(t-var)
is the standard rule for variable in graded systems, where the grade context is the map where the given variable is used once, and no other variable is used. Here, more generally, the variable can get an arbitrary grade
r
, provided that the context is multiplied by
r
. The same “local promotion” can be applied in the following rules in
the top section.

In rule
(t-fun)
, a (possibly recursive) function can get a graded functional type, provided that its body can get the result type in the context enriched by assigning the functional type to the function name, and the parameter type to the parameter. As mentioned, we expect the recursion grade
s
to be
\mathbf{0}
for a non-recursive function; for a recursive function, we expect
s
to be an “infinite” grade, in a sense which will be clarified in
Example

4.2
below.

In the rules in the bottom section, when a value expression is used as subterm of an expression, its grade is required to be non-zero, since it is evaluated in the computation, hence its resource consumption should be taken into account.

In rule
(t-app)
, the function should be produced with a grade which is the sum of that required for the current usage (
r
) and that corresponding to the recursive calls: the latter are the grade required for a single usage multiplied by the recursion grade (
s
). For a non-recursive function (
s=\mathbf{0}
), the rule turns out to be as expected for a usual application.

Example 4.2
.

As an example of type derivation, we consider the function
{\mathsf{div}=\texttt{rec}\,\mathit{f}.\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{y}\texttt{;}\mathit{f}\,{\!\mathit{x}}}
introduced in
Example

3.3
.
In
Fig.

9
, we show a (parametric) proof tree deriving for
\mathsf{div}
a type of the shape
{({\texttt{Unit}}^{r_{1}}\rightarrow_{s}{\texttt{Unit}}^{r_{2}})}
, in a context providing the external resource
\mathit{y}
.

\begin{array}[]{l}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 23.07195pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.90001pt,depth=-2.09999pt,width=46.14389pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-var)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{y}:_{r^{\prime}}\texttt{U}\vdash\mathit{y}:{\texttt{U}}^{r^{\prime}}$}}}}\enskip\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 35.30513pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.90001pt,depth=-2.09999pt,width=70.61026pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-var)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{f}:_{r+r{\cdot}s}\tau\vdash\mathit{f}:{\tau}^{r+r{\cdot}s}$}}}}\enskip\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 30.3565pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.90001pt,depth=-2.09999pt,width=60.71301pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-var)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{x}:_{r{\cdot}r_{1}}\texttt{U}\vdash\mathit{x}:{\texttt{U}}^{r{\cdot}r_{1}}$}}}}\enskip\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.90001pt,depth=-2.09999pt,width=197.22629pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$r\neq\mathbf{0}$}\ \textsc{(t-app)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 49.90407pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{f}:_{r+r{\cdot}s}\tau,\mathit{x}:_{r{\cdot}r_{1}}{\texttt{U}}\vdash\mathit{f}\,{\!\mathit{x}}:{\texttt{U}}^{r{\cdot}r_{2}}$}}}}\enskip\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.90001pt,depth=-2.09999pt,width=331.29295pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$r^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{0}$}\ \textsc{(t-match-u)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 100.48756pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{y}:_{r^{\prime}}\texttt{U},\mathit{f}:_{r+r{\cdot}s}\tau,\mathit{x}:_{r{\cdot}r_{1}}{\texttt{U}}\vdash\mathit{y}\texttt{;}\mathit{f}\,{\!\mathit{x}}:{\texttt{U}}^{r{\cdot}r_{2}}$}}}}\enskip\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.90001pt,depth=-2.09999pt,width=401.56471pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$(r+r{\cdot}s)\preceq s\hskip 8.19447ptr{\cdot}r_{1}\preceq r_{1}\hskip 8.19447ptr_{2}\preceq r{\cdot}r_{2}$}\ \textsc{(t-sub)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 150.11191pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{y}:_{r^{\prime}}\texttt{U},\mathit{f}:_{s}\tau,\mathit{x}:_{r_{1}}{\texttt{U}}\vdash\mathit{y}\texttt{;}\mathit{f}\,{\!\mathit{x}}:{\texttt{U}}^{r_{2}}$}}}}\enskip\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.90001pt,depth=-2.09999pt,width=557.90738pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-fun)}}\hskip 27.12497pt\displaystyle{\tau={\texttt{U}}^{r_{1}}\rightarrow_{s}{\texttt{U}}^{r_{2}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 234.321pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{y}:_{r^{\prime}}\texttt{U}\vdash\texttt{rec}\,\mathit{f}.\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{y}\texttt{;}\mathit{f}\,{\!\mathit{x}}:{\tau}$}}}}\end{array}

Figure 9:
Example of type derivation: recursive function

Consider, first of all, the condition that the recursion grade
s
should satisfy, that is
(r+r{\cdot}s)\preceq s
, for some
r\neq\mathbf{0}
, meaning that it should be enough to cover the recursive call in the body and all the further recursive calls.
6
6

6

Note that
r
is arbitrary, since there is no sound default grade, and only required to be non-zero since the function
is actually used.
.
Assuming the grade algebra of natural numbers of
Example

2.3
(
1
), there is no grade
s
satisfying this condition. In other words, the type system correctly rejects the function since its application would get stuck due to resource consumption, as illustrated in
Example

3.3
. On the other hand, for, e.g.,
s=\infty
, with natural numbers extended as in
Example

2.3
(
7
),
(r+r{\cdot}s)\preceq s
would hold for any
r\neq 0
, hence the function would be well-typed.
Moreover, there would be no constraints on the parameter and return type, since the conditions
r{\cdot}r_{1}\preceq r_{1}
and
r_{2}\preceq r{\cdot}r_{2}
would be always satified taking
r=1
.
Assuming the grade algebra of privacy levels introduced before
Example

3.2
, where
\mathbf{1}=\mathsf{public}
, for
s=\mathsf{public}
the condition is satisfied analogously, again with no constraints on
r_{1}

and
r_{2}
.
For
s=\mathsf{private}
, instead, it only holds for
r=\mathsf{private}
, hence the condition
r_{2}\preceq r{\cdot}r_{2}
prevents the return type of the function to be
\mathsf{public}
, accordingly with the intuition that a function used in
\mathsf{private}
mode cannot return a
\mathsf{public}
result.
In such cases, the type system correctly accepts the function since its application to a value never gets stuck. Finally note that, to type an application of the function, e.g., to derive that

\mathsf{div}\,\,{\!\texttt{u}}
has type
{\texttt{U}}^{r_{2}}
,
\mathsf{div}
should get grade
\mathbf{1}+s
, hence the grade of the external resource
\mathit{y}
should be
(\mathbf{1}+s){\cdot}r^{\prime}
, that is, it should be usable infinitely many times as well.

A similar reasoning applies in general; namely, for recursive calls in a function’s body we get a condition of shape
(r+r{\cdot}s)\preceq s
, with
r\neq\mathbf{0}
, forcing the grade
s
of the function to be “infinite”.
This happens regardless the recursive function is actually always diverging, as in the example above, or terminating on some/all arguments.
On the other hand, in the latter case the resource-aware semantics terminates, as expected, provided that the initial amount of resources is enough to cover the (finite number of) recursive calls. This is perfectly reasonable, the type system being a static overapproximation of the evaluation. We will show an example of this terminating resource-aware evaluation in
Section

5
(
Fig.

13
).

The following lemmas show that the promotion rule, usually explicitly stated in graded type systems, is admissible in our system (
Lemma

4.3
), and also a converse holds for value expressions (
Lemma

4.4
).
Note that we can promote an expression only using a non-zero grade, to ensure that non-zero constraints on grades in typing rules for expressions are preserved.
7
7

7

Without assuming the grade algebra to be integral, we would need to use grades which are not zero-divisors.

These lemmas also show that we can assign to a value expression any grade provided that the context is appropriately adjusted:
by demotion we can always derive
\mathbf{1}
(taking
r=\mathbf{1}
) and then by promotion
any grade.

Lemma 4.3

(Promotion)
.

1.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}:{\tau}^{r}
then

s{\cdot}\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}:{\tau}^{s{\cdot}r}

2.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:{\tau}^{r}
and
s\neq\mathbf{0}
, then

s{\cdot}\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:{\tau}^{s{\cdot}r}
.

Proof.

By induction on the typing rules. We show only some cases.

(t-sub-v)

We have
\Delta\vdash\mathit{v}:{\tau}^{s^{\prime}}
,
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}:{\tau}^{r}
,
r\preceq s^{\prime}
and
\Delta\preceq\Gamma
.
By induction hypothesis
s{\cdot}\Delta\vdash\mathit{v}:{\tau}^{s{\cdot}s^{\prime}}
. By monotonicity
s{\cdot}r\preceq s{\cdot}s^{\prime}
and
s{\cdot}\Delta\preceq s{\cdot}\Gamma
, so, by
(t-sub)
we get
s{\cdot}\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:{\tau}^{s{\cdot}r}
, that is, the thesis.

(t-var)

By rule
(t-var)
we get the thesis.

(t-fun)

We have

\Gamma=r{\cdot}\Gamma^{\prime}
,
\mathit{v}={\texttt{rec}\,\mathit{f}.\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}^{\prime}}
,

\tau={{\tau_{1}}^{r_{1}}\rightarrow_{s^{\prime}}{\tau_{2}}^{r_{2}}}
and

\Gamma^{\prime},\mathit{f}:_{s^{\prime}}\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}\rightarrow_{s^{\prime}}\tau_{2}^{r_{2}},\mathit{x}:_{r_{1}}\tau_{1}\vdash\mathit{e}^{\prime}:\tau_{2}^{r_{2}}
. By rule
(t-fun)
,
(s{\cdot}r){\cdot}\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathit{v}:{\tau}^{s{\cdot}r}
.
From
(s{\cdot}r){\cdot}\Gamma^{\prime}=s{\cdot}(r{\cdot}\Gamma^{\prime})=s{\cdot}\Gamma

we get the thesis.

(t-pair)
,
(t-inl)
and
(t-inr)

Similar to
(t-fun)
.

(t-sub)

We have
\Delta\vdash\mathit{e}:{\tau}^{s^{\prime}}
,
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:{\tau}^{r}
,
r\preceq s^{\prime}
and
\Delta\preceq\Gamma
.
By induction hypothesis
s{\cdot}\Delta\vdash\mathit{e}:{\tau}^{s{\cdot}s^{\prime}}
. By monotonicity
s{\cdot}r\preceq s{\cdot}s^{\prime}
and
s{\cdot}\Delta\preceq s{\cdot}\Gamma
, so, by
(t-sub)
we get
s{\cdot}\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:{\tau}^{s{\cdot}r}
, that is, the thesis.

(t-app)

We have
\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\vdash\mathit{v}_{1}\,{\!\mathit{v}_{2}}:{\tau_{2}}^{r^{\prime}{\cdot}r_{2}}
.
By induction hypothesis
s{\cdot}\Gamma_{1}\vdash\mathit{v}_{1}:{(\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}\rightarrow_{s^{\prime}}\tau_{2}^{r_{2}})}^{s{\cdot}(r^{\prime}+r^{\prime}{\cdot}s^{\prime})}
and
s{\cdot}\Gamma_{2}\vdash\mathit{v}_{2}:{\tau}^{s{\cdot}r^{\prime}{\cdot}r_{1}}
. Since
s\neq\mathbf{0}
and
r^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{0}
and, since the algebra is integral,
s{\cdot}r\neq\mathbf{0}
. By rule
(t-app)
,
s{\cdot}(\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2})\vdash\mathit{v}_{1}\,{\!\mathit{v}_{2}}:{\tau_{2}}^{s{\cdot}r^{\prime}{\cdot}r_{2}}
, that is, the thesis.

(t-match-p)

By induction hypothesis
s{\cdot}\Gamma_{1}\vdash\mathit{v}:{({\tau_{1}}^{r_{1}}\otimes\tau_{2}^{r_{2}})}^{s{\cdot}s^{\prime}}
and
s{\cdot}\Gamma_{2},\mathit{x}:_{(s{\cdot}s^{\prime}){\cdot}r_{1}}\tau,\mathit{y}:_{(s{\cdot}s^{\prime}){\cdot}r_{2}}\tau_{2}\vdash\mathit{e}:{\tau}^{s{\cdot}r}
. Since
s\neq\mathbf{0}
and
s^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{0}
and, since the algebra is integral,
s{\cdot}s^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{0}
. By rule
(t-match-p)
,
s{\cdot}(\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2})\vdash\texttt{match}\ \mathit{v}\ \texttt{with}\ \langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle\rightarrow\mathit{e}:{\tau}^{s{\cdot}r}
, that is, the thesis.

∎

Lemma 4.4

(Demotion)
.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}:{\tau}^{s{\cdot}r}
then

s{\cdot}\Gamma^{\prime}\preceq\Gamma

and
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathit{v}:{\tau}^{r}
, for some
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By case analysis on
\mathit{v}
. We show only some cases.

\mathit{v}=\mathit{x}

By
Lemma

11.1
(
1
)
\mathit{x}:_{r^{\prime}}\tau\preceq\Gamma
and
s{\cdot}r\preceq r^{\prime}
. Let
\Gamma^{\prime}=\mathit{x}:_{r}\tau
.
By monotonicity of
{\cdot}
and, by transitivity of
\preceq
,
(s{\cdot}r){\cdot}\mathit{x}:_{\mathbf{1}}\tau=s{\cdot}\Gamma^{\prime}\preceq\Gamma
. By
(t-var)

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathit{x}:{\tau}^{r}
.

\mathit{v}=\langle^{r_{1}}\mathit{v}_{1},\mathit{v}_{2}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}

By
Lemma

11.1
(
4
)
r^{\prime}{\cdot}(\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2})\preceq\Gamma
and
s{\cdot}r\preceq r^{\prime}
and
\Gamma_{1}\vdash\mathit{v}_{1}:\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}
and
{\Gamma_{2}\vdash\mathit{v}_{2}:\tau_{2}^{r_{2}}}
. Let
\Gamma^{\prime}=r{\cdot}(\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2})
.
By monotonicity of
{\cdot}
and by transitivity of
\preceq
we have
s{\cdot}\Gamma^{\prime}\preceq\Gamma
. By
(t-pair)

{\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathit{v}:{\tau}^{r}}
.

∎

In
Fig.

10
we give the typing rules for environments and configurations.

\begin{array}[]{l}\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-env)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma_{i}\vdash\mathbf{v}_{i}:\tau_{i}^{\mathbf{1}}\quad\forall i\in 1..n}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{x}_{1}:\langle{r_{1}},{\mathbf{v}_{1}}\rangle,\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}:\langle{r_{n}},{\mathbf{v}_{n}}\rangle\triangleright\Delta}\begin{array}[]{l}\Gamma=\mathit{x}_{1}:_{r_{1}}\tau_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}:_{r_{n}}\tau_{n}\\
(r_{1}{\cdot}\Gamma_{1}+\ldots+r_{n}{\cdot}\Gamma_{n}+\Delta)\preceq\Gamma\end{array}\\[12.91663pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-conf)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Delta\vdash\mathit{E}:\mathit{T}\qquad\Gamma\vdash\rho\triangleright\Delta}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{E}\mid\rho:\mathit{T}}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{E}::=\mathit{v}\mid\mathit{e}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 10:
Typing rules for environments and configurations

The typing judgement for environments has shape
\Gamma\vdash\rho\triangleright\Delta
. In rule
(t-env)
, the context
\Gamma
is that
extracted
from the environment, in the sense that, if typechecking succeeds, each variable will get exactly its grade, and the type of its value, in
\rho
. In other words,
\Gamma
gives type and grade information about the resources (variables) provided by the environment.
The context
\Gamma
should be enough to cover both the sum of the contexts needed to typecheck each provided resource, and a
residual
context
\Delta
left for the program (last side condition).
The typing judgment for configurations has shape
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{E}\mid\rho:\mathit{T}
, with
\mathit{E}
either value expression or expression. In rule
(t-conf)
, the residual context obtained by typechecking the environment should be exactly that required by the program.

Remark 4.5
.

Note that, in this way, rule
(t-conf)
imposes
no-waste
.
For instance, taking the linearity grade algebra of
Example

2.3
(
2
),
\Gamma
cannot offer resources which are unused in both the expression and the codomain of the environment.
Hence, e.g.,
\mathit{x}\mid\mathit{x}:\langle{\infty},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle,\mathit{y}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle
, is ill-typed, since the linear resource
\mathit{y}
is wasted, even though in the resource-aware semantics such configuration safely reduces to
\texttt{unit}\mid\mathit{x}:\langle{\infty},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle,\mathit{y}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle
.
In other words, the type system checks that available (non-discardable) resources are fully consumed, whereas in the instrumented semantics there is no such check.
This mismatch between type system and semantics can be eliminated in two ways:

•

as we did in
(
BianchiniDGZ@OOPSLA23
)
, by a more permissive typing rule for configurations, shown below:

\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-conf)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Delta\vdash\mathit{E}:\mathit{T}\qquad\Gamma\vdash\rho\triangleright\widehat{\Gamma}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{E}\mid\rho:\mathit{T}}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{E}::=\mathit{v}\mid\mathit{e}\\
\Delta+\widehat{\Gamma}+\Theta\preceq\Gamma\end{array}

The additional context
\Theta
allows the environment to contain resources which will be wasted; e.g., the configuration
\mathit{x}\mid\mathit{x}:\langle{\infty},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle,\mathit{y}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle
becomes well-typed with
\Theta=\mathit{y}:_{1}\texttt{Unit}
.

•

Conversely, we can refine the instrumented semantics to check no-waste as well; e.g., the configuration
\mathit{x}\mid\mathit{x}:\langle{\infty},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle,\mathit{y}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle
is stuck. This is the approach we will develop in
Section

6
.

5
Programming examples and discussions

In this section, for readability, we use the surface syntax and, moreover, assume some standard additional constructs and syntactic conventions. Notably, we generalize (tensor) product types to tuple types, with the obvious extended syntax, and sum types to variant types, written
{\ell_{1}{:}\mathit{T}_{1}+\ldots+\ell_{n}{:}\mathit{T}_{n}}
for some
tags

\ell_{1}
, …,
\ell_{n}
, injections generalized to tagged variants
\ell^{r}\mathit{e}
, and matching of the shape

\texttt{match}\ \mathit{e}\ \texttt{with}\ \ell_{1}\mathit{x}_{1}\ \texttt{or}\ldots\ \texttt{or}\ \ell_{n}\mathit{x}_{n}
. We write just
\ell
as an abbreviation for an addend
\ell{:}{\texttt{Unit}}^{\mathbf{0}}
in a variant type, and also for the corresponding tagged variants
\ell^{\mathbf{0}}\texttt{unit}
and patterns
\ell\mathit{x}
in a matching construct.
Moreover, we will use type and function definitions (that is, synonyms for function and type expressions), and, as customary, represent (equi-)recursive types
by equations. Finally, we will omit
\mathbf{1}
annotations as in the previous examples, and we will consider
\mathbf{0}
as default, hence omitted, as recursion grade (that is, functions are by default non-recursive).

Examples of type and function definitions

Assume, firstly, the grade algebra of natural numbers with bounded usage,
Example

2.3
(
1
), extended with
\infty
, as in
Example

2.3
(
7
), needed as annotation of recursive functions, as has been illustrated in
Example

4.2
; we discuss below what happens taking exact usage instead.

As a first comment, note that in types of recursive functions the recursion grade needs to be
\infty
, as expected.
On the other hand, most function parameters are graded
1
, since they are used at most once in each branch of the function’s body. The second parameter of multiplication, instead, needs to be graded
\infty
. Indeed, it is used in the body of the function both as argument of sum, and
inside
the recursive call. Hence, its grade
r
should satisfy the equation
(\mathbf{1}+r)\leq r
, analogously to what happens for the recursive grade; compare with the parameter of function
double
, which is used twice as well, and can be graded
2
.
In the following alternative definitions:

⬇

double
:

Nat
1

->

Nat

double

n

=

n

*

succ

succ

zero

double
:

Nat
∞

->

Nat

double

n

=

succ

succ

zero

*

n

the parameter needs to be graded differently depending on how it is used in the multiplication. In other words, the resource-aware type system captures, as expected, non-extensional properties.

Assume now the grade algebra of natural numbers with exact usage, again extended with
\infty
. Interestingly enough, the functions
length
and
get
above are no longer typable.

In
length
, this is due to the fact that, when the list is non-empty, the head is unused, whereas, since the grade of a pair is “propagated” to both the components, it should be used exactly once as the tail.
The function would be typable assuming for lists the type
NatList
=
empty
+
cons
:(
Nat
{}^{\mathbf{0}}\otimes
NatList
)
, which, however, would mean to essentially handle a list as a natural number.

Function
get
, analogously, cannot be typed since, in the last line, only one component of a non-empty list (either the head or the tail) is used in a branch of the match, whereas both should be used exactly once.
Both functions could be typed, instead, grading with
\infty
the list parameter; this would mean to allow an arbitrary usage in the body. These examples suggest that, in a grade algebra with exact usage, such as that of natural numbers, or the simpler linearity grade algebra, see
Example

2.3
(
2
), there is often no middle way between imposing severe limitations on code, to ensure linearity (or, in general, exactness), and allowing code which is essentially non-graded.

Additive product

The product type we consider in
Fig.

7
is the
tensor
product, also called
multiplicative
, following Linear Logic terminology
(
Girard87
)
.
In the destructor construct for such type, both components are simultaneously extracted, each one with a grade which is (a multiple of) that of the pair, see the semantic
8
8

8

Typing rules follow the same pattern.
rule
(match-p)
in
Fig.

5
. Thus, as shown in the examples above, programs which discard the use of either component cannot be typed in a non-affine grade algebra. Correspondingly, the resource consumption for constructing a (multiplicative) pair is the
sum
of those for constructing the two components, corresponding to a sequential evaluation, see rule
(pair)
in
Fig.

5
. The
cartesian
product, instead, also called
additive
, formalized in
Fig.

12
, has one destructor for each component, so that which is not extracted is discarded. Correspondingly, the resource consumption for constructing an additive pair is
an upper bound
of those for constructing the two components, corresponding in a sense to a non-deterministic evaluation.
The
get
example, rewritten using the constructs of the additive product, becomes typable even in a non-affine grade algebra.
In an affine grade algebra, programs can always be rewritten replacing the cartesian product with the tensor, and conversely; in particular,
\pi_{i}\mathit{v}
can be encoded as

\texttt{match}\ \mathit{v}\ \texttt{with}\ \langle\mathit{x}_{1},\mathit{x}_{2}\rangle\rightarrow\mathit{x}_{i}
, even though, as said above, resources are consumed differently (sum versus upper bound). An interesting remark is that record/object calculi, where generally width subtyping is allowed,
meaning that components can be discarded at runtime, and object construction happens by sequential evaluation of the fields, need to be modeled by multiplicative product and affine grades. In future work we plan to investigate object calculi which are
linear
, or, more generally, use resources in
an exact way.

Terminating recursion

As anticipated, even though the type system can only derive, for recursive functions, recursion grades which are “infinite”, their calls which terminate in standard semantics can terminate also in resource-aware semantics, provided that the initial amount of the function resource is enough to cover the (finite number of) recursive calls, as shown in
Fig.

13
.

For brevity, we write
z
,
s
, and
t
for
zero
,
succ
, and
true
, respectively,
\texttt{if-z(}\mathit{v},\mathit{e}_{1},\texttt{s}\,{\!\mathit{x}}{\rightarrow}\mathit{e}_{2}\texttt{)}
for
\texttt{match}\ \mathit{v}\ \texttt{with}\ \texttt{z}\,\rightarrow\mathit{e}_{1}\ \texttt{or}\ \texttt{s}\,{\!\mathit{x}}\rightarrow\mathit{e}_{2}
, and
\langle{r}{,}{s}{,}{\mathbf{v}}\rangle
for the environment
{\texttt{ev}\,:\langle{r},{\texttt{even}}\rangle,\texttt{n}:\langle{s},{\mathbf{v}}\rangle}
.

\begin{array}[]{l}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\hskip 3.6806pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 36.818pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.13475pt,depth=-1.54585pt,width=73.63599pt\hbox{\kern 2.0417pt$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(var)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\texttt{ev}\,\mid\langle{1}{,}{1}{,}{\texttt{z}}\rangle\Rightarrow_{1}\texttt{even}\mid\langle{0}{,}{1}{,}{\texttt{z}}\rangle$}}}}\hskip 3.6806pt\penalty\par\par\hskip 3.6806pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 29.2085pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.13475pt,depth=-1.54585pt,width=58.41699pt\hbox{\kern 2.0417pt$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(var)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\texttt{n}\mid\langle{0}{,}{1}{,}{\texttt{z}}\rangle\Rightarrow\texttt{z}\mid\langle{0}{,}{0}{,}{\texttt{z}}\rangle$}}}}\hskip 3.6806pt\penalty\par\par\hskip 3.6806pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 26.6665pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\texttt{t}\mid\langle{0}{,}{0}{,}{\texttt{z}}\rangle\Rightarrow\texttt{t}\mid\langle{0}{,}{0}{,}{\texttt{z}}\rangle$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.13475pt,depth=-1.54585pt,width=111.75002pt\hbox{\kern 2.0417pt$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(match-l)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\texttt{if-z(}\texttt{z},\texttt{t},\texttt{s}\,{\!\texttt{n}}{\rightarrow}\texttt{not}\,{\!(\texttt{ev}\,\,{\!\texttt{n}})}\texttt{)}\mid\langle{0}{,}{0}{,}{\texttt{z}}\rangle\Rightarrow\texttt{t}\mid\langle{0}{,}{0}{,}{\texttt{z}}\rangle$}}}}\hskip 3.6806pt\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.13475pt,depth=-1.54585pt,width=295.2394pt\hbox{\kern 2.0417pt$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(app)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 115.36957pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\texttt{ev}\,\,{\!\texttt{n}}\mid\langle{1}{,}{1}{,}{\texttt{z}}\rangle\Rightarrow\texttt{t}\mid\langle{0}{,}{0}{,}{\texttt{z}}\rangle$}}}}\par\\[15.0694pt]

Processing data from/to files

The following example illustrates how we can simultaneously track privacy levels, as introduced in
Example

3.2
, and linearity information. Linearity grades guarantee the correct use of files, whereas privacy levels are used to ensure that data are handled without leaking information.
We combine the two grade algebras with the smash product of
Example

2.8
.
So there are five grades:
0
(meaning unused),
\mathsf{priv}{.}1
and
\mathsf{pub}{.}1
(meaning used linearly in either
\mathsf{priv}
or
\mathsf{pub}
mode), and
\mathsf{priv}{.}\omega
,
\mathsf{pub}{.}\omega
(meaning used an arbitrary number of times in either
\mathsf{priv}
or
\mathsf{pub}
mode). The partial order is graphically shown in
Fig.

15
. The neutral element for multiplication is
\mathsf{pub}{.}1
, which therefore will be omitted.

Figure 16:
Processing data from/to files

The function
fileRW

takes as parameters an input and an output file handler and a function that processes the character read from the input file. The result indicates whether all the characters of the input file have been successfully processed and written in the output file or there was an error in processing some character.

The function starts by reading a character from the input file.
If
read
returns
none
, then all the characters from the input file have been read and so both files are closed and the function returns
success
(lines 5—6). Closing the files is necessary in order to typecheck this branch of the match.
If
read
returns a character (lines 7—12), then the processing function
fn
is applied to that character. Then, if
fn
returns a character, then this result is written to the output file using the file handler passed as a parameter and, finally, the function is recursively called with the file handlers returned by the
read
and
write
functions as arguments. If, instead,
fn
returns
error
, then both files are closed and the function returns
failure
(lines 11—12).

Observe that, given the order of
Fig.

15
, we have
0\not\preceq\mathsf{pub}{.}1
. Therefore the variables of type
FileHandle
, which have grade
\mathsf{pub}{.}1
, must be used exactly once in every branch of the matches in their scope.

A call to
fileRW
could be:
fileRW

(
open

"
inFile
")

(
open

"
outFile
")

(
rec

f
.\
x
.
x
)
.

Note that , with

⬇
