:

(
Char
{}^{\mathsf{pub}{.}\omega}\otimes

FileHandle
)

\rightarrow

FileHandle

the function
fileRW
would not be well-typed, since a
\mathsf{priv}
character cannot be the input of
write
. On the other hand, using subsumption, we can apply
fileRW
to a processing function with type

⬇

Char
{}^{\mathsf{priv}{.}\omega}\rightarrow

(
ok
:
Char
pub.ω
+

error
)

Finally, in the type of
fileRW
, the grade of the first arrow says that the function is recursive and it is internally used in an unrestricted way. The function could also be typed with:

⬇

FileHandle

\rightarrow_{\mathsf{priv}{.}\omega}

FileHandle

\rightarrow

fnType
{}^{\mathsf{pub}{.}\omega}\rightarrow

Result
priv.ω

However, in this case its final result would be private and therefore less usable.

6
No-waste semantics

This section is structured as follows. We first informally describe no-waste usage in
Section

6.1
. To formalize this notion, we need definitions and results related to
grade graphs
, provided in
Section

6.2
. In
Section

6.3
we define the no-waste refinement of the resource-aware semantics, and examples of reduction are shown in
Section

6.4
.
Finally, in
Section

6.5
we prove the related results, the most important one being that the refined semantics actually ensures no-waste (
Theorem

6.13
).

6.1
No-waste usage

In the resource-aware semantics in
Fig.

5
, a program cannot consume more resources than those available. That is, reduction is stuck if the current availability of a resource is not enough to cover the required usage by the program, and this situation is expected to be prevented by the type system.

However, the converse property does not hold, that is, resources can be
wasted
, even though, see
Remark

4.5
at the end of
Section

4
, the type system prevents this situation as well. For instance, assuming linearity grades, the configuration
\mathit{x}\mid\mathit{x}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle,\mathit{y}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle
, where both
\mathit{x}
and
\mathit{y}
are provided as linear, reduces (with grade
1
) to
\texttt{unit}\mid\mathit{y}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle
, by wasting the resource
\mathit{y}
. In this section, we provide a refinement of resource-aware semantics where
resources cannot be wasted
, hence the configuration above is stuck. In this way, resource-aware soundness including no-waste can be stated and proved.

To formally express that “resources cannot be wasted”, our first step is to define a relation
\gamma\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\rho
, meaning that the resources (transitively) used by
\gamma
are covered,
with no-waste, by
\rho
.

In the simple case when values in the environment are closed, as in the example above, there is no need of transitivity; for instance,
\mathit{x}:1,\mathit{y}:1\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\mathit{x}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle,\mathit{y}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle
holds, whereas
\mathit{x}:1,\mathit{y}:0\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\mathit{x}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle,\mathit{y}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle
does not hold.
On the other hand, when values have free variables, dependencies in the environment should be taken into account. To this end, as shown in
Fig.

17
, the no-waste semantics is instrumented by
annotating
values with a grade context “declaring” the
direct usage
of resources in the value, so that transitive usage can be computed.

\begin{array}[]{lcll}\rho&::=&\mathit{x}_{1}:\langle{r_{1}},{\mathcal{V}_{1}}\rangle,\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}:\langle{r_{n}},{\mathcal{V}_{n}}\rangle&\mbox{environment}\\
\mathcal{V}&::=&\mathbf{v}^{\gamma}&\mbox{(annotated) value}\\
\gamma&::=&\mathit{x}_{1}:r_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}:r_{n}&\mbox{grade context}\\
\mathit{E}&::=&\mathit{v}\mid\mathit{e}&\mbox{value expression or expression}\end{array}

Figure 17:
Environments and (annotated) values

For instance,
\mathit{x}:1\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\mathit{x}:\langle{1},{(\lambda\_.\langle\mathit{y},\mathit{z}\mathchoice{\hphantom{}\rangle^{{\kern-3.50002pt{}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{}\rangle^{{\kern-3.50002pt{}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{}\rangle^{{\kern-3.50002pt{}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{}\rangle^{{\kern-3.50002pt{}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}})^{\mathit{y}:1,\mathit{z}:1},\mathit{y}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}^{\emptyset}}\rangle,\mathit{z}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}^{\emptyset}}\rangle}\rangle
holds, since
\mathit{x}:1
directly uses
\mathit{x}
, and transitively
\mathit{y}
and
\mathit{z}
, exactly once. Note that the relation of no-waste usage, being based on the partial order, still allows approximation which is no-waste, see
Remark

4.1
.
Hence, for instance,
\mathit{x}:1\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\mathit{x}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}^{\emptyset}}\rangle,\mathit{y}:\langle{\infty},{\texttt{unit}^{\emptyset}}\rangle
holds, since the resource
\mathit{y}
, which is not used by the grade context, is discardable.

Set
\mathsf{fv}(\mathbf{v})
the free variables of
\mathbf{v}
, defined in the standard way.
We assume environments to be
closed
, that is, for each
\mathit{x}:\langle{r},{\mathbf{v}^{\gamma}}\rangle\in\rho
,
\mathsf{fv}(\mathbf{v})\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\rho)
.

The grade context annotating a value is meant to be a “guess” about the resource consumption which would be actually triggered when the value is used. Reduction will be non-stuck when the guess is correct (and no standard typing errors occur); as expected, the type system will check such correctness. We only assume that, in an annotated value
\mathbf{v}^{\gamma}
,
\gamma
is
honest
for
\mathbf{v}
, that is, resources declared with a non-discardable amount in
\gamma
should at least occur in
\mathbf{v}
, as formally defined below:

Definition 6.1

(Honest)
.

A grade context
\gamma
is honest for
\mathit{E}
if
\mathbf{0}\not\preceq\gamma(\mathit{x})
implies
\mathit{x}\in\mathsf{fv}(\mathit{E})
.

For instance,
(\lambda\_.\mathit{y})^{\mathit{y}:1,\mathit{z}:1}
is an ill-formed value. The honesty condition is intuitively reasonable, and allows us to prove the important property that garbage can be discarded (
Proposition

6.16
).

Given an environment
\rho=\mathit{x}_{1}:\langle{r_{1}},{\mathcal{V}_{1}}\rangle,\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}:\langle{r_{n}},{\mathcal{V}_{n}}\rangle
, we can extract from
\rho
the grade context
\gamma_{\rho}=\mathit{x}_{1}:r_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}:r_{n}
. Moreover, it is intuitively clear that we can extract from
\rho
a graph
\mathsf{G}_{\rho}
expressing (direct) dependencies among variables.
This graph will be used in the formal definition of the relation
\gamma\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\rho
to take into account transitive usages.
Before introducing this notion, however, we need some technical developments about this kind of graphs, provided in
Section

6.2
. Then we will come back to the no-waste property in
Section

6.3
.

6.2
Grade graphs: an algebraic digression

Recall that we denote by
\mathsf{V}
the set of variables and that, for a function
f:X\rightarrow|\mathit{R}|
, the support of
f
is the set

\mathsf{S}_{f}=\{x\in X\mid f(x)\neq\mathbf{0}\}
.

Definition 6.2

(Grade graph)
.

A
grade graph
is a function
\mathsf{G}:\mathsf{V}\times\mathsf{V}\rightarrow|\mathit{R}|
with finite support.

The finite support condition ensures that

\mathsf{G}(x,y)\neq\mathbf{0}
for finitely many pairs
\langle x,y\rangle\in\mathsf{V}\times\mathsf{V}
.
As the name suggests,
these functions can be represented by finite graphs where nodes are variables and edges are labeled by grades,
analogously to functions from variables into grades with finite support which are represented by grade contexts.
More precisely, given a grade graph
\mathsf{G}
,
we have an edge from
\mathit{x}
to
y
with label
r
exactly when
\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=r
.
Intuitively, such an edge means that
\mathit{x}
depends on “
r
copies” of
\mathit{y}
; for
r=\mathbf{0}
,
\mathit{x}
does
not
depend on
\mathit{y}
, and the edge can be omitted.
Finally, again following graph terminology, we will call
source
in
\mathsf{G}
a node (variable)
\mathit{y}
such that
\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=\mathbf{0}
for all
\mathit{x}
.
We will write
V(\mathsf{G})
for the set of
non-source
nodes, i.e., those with at least one incoming edge:

V(\mathsf{G})=\{\mathit{y}\in\mathsf{V}\mid\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})\neq\mathbf{0}\text{ for some }\mathit{x}\in\mathsf{V}\}

Since
\mathsf{G}
has finite support, the set
V(\mathsf{G})
is clearly finite, as its cardinality is bounded
by that of
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{G}}
.

In order to model transitive dependencies, we have to consider paths in a grade graph.
The next definition introduces path-related notions, adapting them from the standard graph-theoretic ones.

Definition 6.3

(Paths, cycles and cyclic grade graphs)
.

A
path
is a sequence
\mathit{p}=\mathit{x}_{1}\ldots\mathit{x}_{n}
, with
n\geq 1
;
\mathit{x}_{1},\mathit{x}_{n}
are denoted
\mathsf{in}(\mathit{p})
and
\mathsf{out}(\mathit{p})
, respectively.
We set
{\mid}\mathit{p}{\mid}=n-1
the
length
of the path
\mathit{p}
.
9
9

9

Note that the length counts the number of edges which is the number of nodes minus
1
.

A path
\mathit{x}_{1}\ldots\mathit{x}_{n}
is a
cycle
if
n>1
and
\mathit{x}_{1}=\mathit{x}_{n}
.
A grade graph
\mathsf{G}
induces a function
\mathsf{G}:\mathsf{V}^{+}\rightarrow\mathit{R}
inductively defined as follows:

\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x})=\mathbf{1}

\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x}\cdot\mathit{p})=\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x},\mathsf{in}(\mathit{p})){\cdot}\mathsf{G}(\mathit{p})

Then,
\mathsf{G}
is
acyclic
if, for all cycles
\mathit{p}
,
\mathsf{G}(\mathit{p})=\mathbf{0}
.

We will write
\mathsf{P}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})
for the set of paths
\mathit{p}
such that

\mathsf{in}(\mathit{p})=\mathit{x}
and
\mathsf{out}(\mathit{p})=\mathit{y}
.
The following are immediate consequences of the above definitions.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a grade graph and
\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\in\mathsf{V}
.
Set

\mathsf{P}_{{\neq}\mathbf{0}}^{\mathsf{G}}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=\{\mathit{p}\in\mathsf{P}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})\mid\mathsf{G}(\mathit{p})\neq\mathbf{0}\}
.

1.

If
\mathit{p}=\mathit{x}_{1}\cdots\mathit{x}_{n+1}\in\mathsf{P}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})
with
n\geq 1
and there is
j\in 2..n+1
such that
\mathit{x}_{j}\notin V(\mathsf{G})
, then
\mathsf{G}(\mathit{p})=\mathbf{0}
.

2.

If
\mathit{x}\neq\mathit{y}
and
\mathit{y}\notin V(\mathsf{G})
, then
\mathsf{P}_{{\neq}\mathbf{0}}^{\mathsf{G}}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=\emptyset
.

3.

If
\mathsf{G}
is acyclic and
n
is the cardinality of
V(\mathsf{G})
, then
every path
\mathit{p}
with
{\mid}\mathit{p}{\mid}\geq n+1
is such that
\mathsf{G}(\mathit{p})=\mathbf{0}
.

4.

If
\mathsf{G}
is acyclic, then the set

\mathsf{P}_{{\neq}\mathbf{0}}^{\mathsf{G}}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})
is finite.

For acyclic grade graphs we can define the reflexive and transitive closure, as detailed below.

Definition 6.5

(Reflexive and transitive closure of a grade graph)
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be an acyclic grade graph.
Its
reflexive and transitive closure
, denoted
{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}
, is defined as follows:

for all
\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\in\mathsf{V}
,

\displaystyle{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=\sum_{\mathit{p}\in\mathsf{P}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})}\mathsf{G}(\mathit{p})

The definition of
{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}(x,y)
, for each pair of nodes
x,y
, requires a summation over an infinite set.
However,
Proposition

6.4
(
4
) ensures that only finitely many addends are different from
\mathbf{0}
, hence the summation is well-defined.

Note that the reflexive and transitive closure of a grade graph
\mathsf{G}
is not acyclic nor a grade graph.
Indeed, we have
{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}(\mathit{x},\mathit{x})=\mathbf{1}
for all
\mathit{x}\in\mathsf{V}
, hence
cycles of the form
\mathit{x}\cdot\mathit{x}
have a non-zero grade and
\mathsf{S}_{{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}}
is infinite.
However, it still has good properties,
as described below.

Let
M:\mathsf{V}\times\mathsf{V}\rightarrow|\mathit{R}|
be a function.
For every variable
\mathit{x}\in\mathsf{V}
, we define the function
M_{\mathit{x}}:\mathsf{V}\rightarrow|\mathit{R}|
as follows:

M_{\mathit{x}}(\mathit{y})=M(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})
.

A
grade matrix
is a function
M:\mathsf{V}\times\mathsf{V}\rightarrow|\mathit{R}|
such that, for every variable
\mathit{x}\in\mathsf{V}
,

M_{\mathit{x}}
has a finite support, i.e., it is a grade context.
Roughly, grade matrices are very much like grade graphs except that, instead of requiring only finitely many non-isolated nodes, they only require that every node has finitely many non-zero outgoing edges.
It is immediate to see that grade graphs are a special case of grade matrices.
Moreover, thanks to
Proposition

6.4
(
2
), we have that
{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}
is a grade matrix.
Indeed, it entails that, for all variables
\mathit{x}\in\mathsf{V}
, we have

\mathsf{S}_{{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}_{\mathit{x}}}\subseteq V(\mathsf{G})\cup\{\mathit{x}\}
, which thus is finite.

Grade matrices carry a rich algebraic structure.
First of all, they carry an ordered commutative monoid structure, given by the pointwise extension of the addition and ordering on grades.
Moreover, we can define another ordered monoid structure, given by the standard row-by-colum multiplication.
To this end, we first define the multiplication of a grade matrix by a single row, i.e., a grade context, on the left.
Given a grade context
\gamma
and a grade matrix
M
, the grade context
\gamma{\cdot}M
is defined by
10
10

10

Note that this corresponds to vector-matrix multiplication.
:

for all
\mathit{x}
,
\displaystyle(\gamma{\cdot}M)(\mathit{x})=\sum_{\mathit{y}\in\mathsf{V}}\gamma(\mathit{y})\cdot M(\mathit{y},\mathit{x})

Note that the infinite summation above has only finitely many non-zero addends, as
\gamma
has a finite support, hence it is well-defined.
Moreover, it is easy to see that

\gamma{\cdot}M=\sum_{\mathit{x}\in\mathsf{S}_{\gamma}}\gamma(\mathit{x}){\cdot}M_{\mathit{x}}
, hence it is a well-defined grade context. Now, given matrices
M_{1},M_{2}
, we set

for all
\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\in\mathsf{V}
,

\displaystyle(M_{1}\cdot M_{2})(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=({M_{1}}_{\mathit{x}}{\cdot}M_{2})(\mathit{y})=\sum_{z\in\mathsf{V}}M_{1}(\mathit{x},z){\cdot}M_{2}(z,\mathit{y})

This is obviously well-defined because we have

(M_{1}{\cdot}M_{2})_{\mathit{x}}={M_{1}}_{\mathit{x}}{\cdot}M_{2}
which has a finite support, being a grade context.
It is easy to see that this operation is associative and monotone and has as neutral element the identity grade matrix
\mathbb{I}
defined by

\mathbb{I}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=\begin{cases}\mathbf{1}&\mbox{if}\ \mathit{x}=\mathit{y}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases}

Given a grade graph
\mathsf{G}
and a variable
\mathit{x}\in\mathsf{V}
,
the grade context
\mathsf{G}_{\mathit{x}}

corresponds to the “row” expressing the direct dependencies of
\mathit{x}
.
The following lemma states that, being the graph acyclic, multiplying such row with the matrix expressing transitive dependencies we get zero on the component corresponding to the variable
\mathit{x}
itself.
Equivalently, the matrix obtained by multiplying the graph
\mathsf{G}
with the matrix of transitive dependencies has
\mathbf{0}
on the diagonal.
Intuitively, this happens because the grade matrix
\mathsf{G}{\cdot}{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}
considers transitive dependencies through non-empty paths and, being the graph acyclic, there are no non-empty paths from a variable to itself.

Lemma 6.6
.

If
\mathsf{G}
is acyclic, then

(\mathsf{G}{\cdot}{\mathsf{G}}^{\star})(\mathit{x},\mathit{x})=(\mathsf{G}_{\mathit{x}}{\cdot}{\mathsf{G}}^{\star})(\mathit{x})=\mathbf{0}
.

The connection between multiplication by a grade graph and its paths is very deep, as the following proposition shows.
Given a grade graph
\mathsf{G}
, in the following we will write

\mathsf{G}^{n}
to denote the multiplication of
\mathsf{G}
with itself
n
times.
Moreover, we will denote by
\mathsf{P}_{n}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})
the subset of
\mathsf{P}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})
of those paths of length
n
.

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a grade graph.
For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\in\mathsf{V}
, we have

\mathsf{G}^{n}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=\sum_{\mathit{p}\in\mathsf{P}_{n}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})}\mathsf{G}(\mathit{p})
.

Combining
Proposition

6.7
and
Proposition

6.4
(
3
),
it is immediate to see that, if
n
is the cardinality of
V(\mathsf{G})
, then

\mathsf{G}^{k}
is the zero grade matrix, for all
k\geq n+1
.
As a consequence we get the following result.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be an acyclic grade graph and let
n
be the cardinality of
V(\mathsf{G})
.
Then the following holds:

{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\mathsf{G}^{i}

Proof.

Using
Proposition

6.7
, we get the following

{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=\sum_{\mathit{p}\in\mathsf{P}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y}))}\mathsf{G}_{(}\mathit{p})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{\mathit{p}\in\mathsf{P}_{k}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})}\mathsf{G}(\mathit{p})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathsf{G}^{k}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})

As already noticed, by
Proposition

6.4
(
3
), we have that

\mathsf{G}^{k}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=\mathbf{0}
for all
k\geq n+1
, hence we have the thesis.
∎

Corollary 6.9
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be an acyclic grade graph.
Then,
{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}=\mathbb{I}+{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}{\cdot}\mathsf{G}=\mathbb{I}+\mathsf{G}{\cdot}{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}
.

Proof.

Let
n
be the cardinality of
V(\mathsf{G})
.
The thesis is immediate by
Lemma

6.8
:

\mathbb{I}+{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}{\cdot}\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{G}^{0}+\sum_{k\in 0..n}\mathsf{G}^{k+1}=\sum_{k\in 0..n}\mathsf{G}^{k}+\mathsf{G}^{n+1}={\mathsf{G}}^{\star}

because
\mathsf{G}^{n+1}
is the zero grade matrix.
∎

Another key property of the reflexive and transitive closure of a grade graph is that it satisfies an induction principle
expressed by
Proposition

6.10
.
It essentially ensures that, for every grade context
\gamma_{1}
, the monotone function on grade contexts

\phi_{\gamma_{1}}(\gamma)=\gamma_{1}+\gamma{\cdot}\mathsf{G}

has a least pre-fixed point given by
\gamma_{1}{\cdot}{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}
.
Actually, this is also a fixed point thanks to
Corollary

6.9
.

Proposition 6.10
.

Given an acyclic graph
\mathsf{G}
, and two grade contexts
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
:

\gamma+(\gamma^{\prime}{\cdot}\mathsf{G})\preceq\gamma^{\prime}
implies
\gamma{\cdot}\mathsf{G}^{\star}\preceq\gamma^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By a straightforward induction on
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we can prove that

\gamma_{1}{\cdot}\left(\sum_{i\in 0..k}\mathsf{G}^{k}\right)+\gamma_{2}{\cdot}\mathsf{G}^{k+1}\preceq\gamma_{2}
,
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Taking
n
to be the cardinality of
V(\mathsf{G})
, by
Lemma

6.8
, we get the thesis because

{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}=\sum_{i\in 0..n}\mathsf{G}^{i}
and
\mathsf{G}^{n+1}
is the zero matrix.
∎

6.3
No-waste semantics

We are now ready to formally define the graph
\mathsf{G}_{\rho}
extracted from an (augmented) environment
\rho
:

\mathsf{G}_{\rho}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=r
if
\rho(\mathit{x})=\langle{\_},{\_^{\gamma}}\rangle
,
\gamma(\mathit{y})=r

\mathsf{G}_{\rho}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=\mathbf{0}
if
\mathit{x}\not\in\mathsf{dom}(\rho)

In the following, we will assume acyclic environments, in the sense that there is no dependency path from a variable to itself.
Formally, this means that we assume that the grade graph
\mathsf{G}_{\rho}
is acyclic (see
Definition

6.3
). It is important to note this
does not
prevent provided resources to be recursive; indeed, this is handled in the semantics by adding a resource, under a fresh name, for each recursive call, as seen in rule
(app)
in
Fig.

5
and the example in
Fig.

13
.
We discuss later whether allowing cyclic environments rather than modeling recursion, essentially, by unfolding, makes some relevant difference, and how to adjust the technical treatment.

As the reader can expect, the reflexive and transitive closure
\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho}
of the grade graph extracted from an environment
\rho
expresses the
(full) dependencies
among resources in
\mathsf{dom}(\rho)
. Indeed, for each
\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\in\mathsf{dom}(\rho)
, a dependency path from
\mathit{x}
to
\mathit{y}
expresses
one
transitive (graded) usage of
\mathit{y}
from (the value associated to)
\mathit{x}
; thus, the sum of the weights of such paths represents the whole usage of
\mathit{y}
from
\mathit{x}
.

We can finally define the relation between grade contexts and environments modeling no-waste usage.

Definition 6.11

(No-waste usage)
.

The context
\gamma

(transitively) uses
\rho
with no waste
, written
\gamma\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\rho
, if
\gamma{\cdot}{\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho}}\preceq\gamma_{\rho}
.

Indeed, for each resource
\mathit{x}
,
(\gamma{\cdot}{\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho}})(\mathit{x})
gives the sum of the usages of
\mathit{x}
from any variable
\mathit{y}
, each one multiplied by the direct usage of
\mathit{x}
in
\gamma
.

We can now formally define the no-waste semantics. The reduction judgement for expressions has shape
\mathit{e}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho^{\prime}
, and is defined in
Fig.

18
on top of an analogous one for value expressions. As noted for the previous semantics in
Fig.

5
, for simplicity we use the same notation for the two judgments, and in the bottom section of
Fig.

18
we write both judgments as premises.

In the reduction rules,
\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}
denotes the
sum
of environments, defined as follows, analogously to the sum of contexts in
Section

4
:

\displaystyle\emptyset+\rho

\displaystyle=\rho

\displaystyle(\mathit{x}:\langle{s},{\mathcal{V}}\rangle,\rho_{1})+(\mathit{x}:\langle{r},{\mathcal{V}}\rangle,\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathit{x}:\langle{s+r},{\mathcal{V}}\rangle,(\rho_{1}+\rho_{2})

\displaystyle(\mathit{x}:\langle{s},{\mathcal{V}}\rangle,\rho_{1})+\rho_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathit{x}:\langle{s},{\mathcal{V}}\rangle,(\rho_{1}+\rho_{2})

if
\mathit{x}\notin\mathsf{dom}(\rho_{2})

We use an analogous notation for grade contexts. Note that, as sum of contexts, sum of environments is partial, since a variable in the
domain of both is required to have
the same associated value.

\begin{array}[]{l}\scriptstyle{\textsc{(var)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{\mathit{x}\mid\rho,\mathit{x}:\langle{s},{\mathcal{V}}\rangle\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho,\mathit{x}:\langle{s^{\prime}},{\mathcal{V}}\rangle}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{V}=\mathbf{v}^{\gamma}\\
r+s^{\prime}\preceq s\\
r{\cdot}\gamma\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\rho,\mathit{x}:\langle{s^{\prime}},{\mathcal{V}}\rangle\end{array}\\[19.37492pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(fun)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{\texttt{rec}\,\mathit{f}.\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}(\texttt{rec}\,\mathit{f}.\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e})^{\gamma}\mid\rho}\begin{array}[]{l}r{\cdot}\gamma\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\rho\end{array}\hskip 18.49988pt\scriptstyle{\textsc{(unit)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{}{\texttt{unit}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\texttt{unit}^{\gamma}\mid\rho}\begin{array}[]{l}r{\cdot}\gamma\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\rho\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(pair)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathit{v}_{1}\mid\rho_{1}\Rrightarrow_{r{\cdot}r_{1}}\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}\mid\rho_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{v}_{2}\mid\rho_{2}\Rrightarrow_{r{\cdot}r_{2}}\mathbf{v}_{2}^{\gamma_{2}}\mid\rho_{2}^{\prime}}{\langle^{r_{1}}\mathit{v}_{1},\mathit{v}_{2}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.22601pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.22601pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.62254pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.62254pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\langle^{r_{1}}\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.22601pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.22601pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.62254pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.62254pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}^{\gamma}\mid\rho_{1}^{\prime}+\rho_{2}^{\prime}}\begin{array}[]{l}\gamma=r_{1}{\cdot}\gamma_{1}+r_{2}{\cdot}\gamma_{2}\\
\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}\preceq\rho\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(inl)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r{\cdot}s}\mathbf{v}^{\gamma}\mid\rho^{\prime}}{\texttt{inl}^{r}\mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}(\texttt{inl}^{r}\mathbf{v})^{s{\cdot}\gamma}\mid\rho^{\prime}}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\hskip 18.49988pt\scriptstyle{\textsc{(inr)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r{\cdot}s}\mathbf{v}^{\gamma}\mid\rho^{\prime}}{\texttt{inr}^{r}\mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}(\texttt{inr}^{r}\mathbf{v})^{s{\cdot}\gamma}\mid\rho^{\prime}}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{l}\\
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(ret)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{s}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho^{\prime}}{\texttt{return}\ \mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho^{\prime}}\begin{array}[]{l}r\preceq s\neq\mathbf{0}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]

\mathit{x}^{\prime}\ \mbox{fresh}\end{array}\par\\[15.49994pt]

s_{1}\neq\mathbf{0}\\
\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}\preceq\rho\\
\mathit{f}^{\prime},\mathit{x}^{\prime}\ \mbox{fresh}\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]

\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}\preceq\rho\end{array}\\[15.49994pt]

\mathit{e}[\mathit{x}^{\prime}/\mathit{x}][\mathit{y}^{\prime}/\mathit{y}]\mid\rho,\mathit{x}^{\prime}:\langle{s{\cdot}r_{1}},{\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}}\rangle,\mathit{y}^{\prime}:\langle{s{\cdot}r_{2}},{\mathbf{v}_{2}^{\gamma_{2}}}\rangle\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho^{\prime}\end{array}}{\texttt{match}\ \mathit{v}\ \texttt{with}\ \langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle\rightarrow\mathit{e}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho^{\prime}}\begin{array}[]{l}s\neq\mathbf{0}\\

\gamma\preceq s{\cdot}\gamma_{1}\\
\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}\preceq\rho\\
\mathit{y}\ \mbox{fresh}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 18:
No-waste semantics

The distinguishing features with respect to the previous resource-aware semantics are the following:

•

in axioms, we impose to obtain
only no-waste results
, that is, resources left in the environment should be those used, with no waste, by the (grade context annotating the) value

•

in particular, when replacing a variable occurrence, only a discardable amount could be left

•

when reducing a term with more than one subterm, the available resources are
split
among subterms, analogously to what happens in resource-aware type systems.

We explain now the single rules, focusing on the novel features summarized above.

When replacing a variable occurrence by the associated value, as in the previous rule in
Fig.

5
, its current amount should be enough to cover that required by the reduction grade (second side condition). However, there is the additional side condition that the result should be no-waste; since it is produced in
r
copies, this condition should hold for a grade context obtained by multiplying by
r
that declared in the annotated value.

Note that this condition implies that the residual amount
s^{\prime}

should be discardable
. Indeed, set
\rho^{\prime}=\rho,\mathit{x}:\langle{s^{\prime}},{\mathcal{V}}\rangle
, we know that
(r{\cdot}\gamma){\cdot}{\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho^{\prime}}}\preceq\gamma_{\rho^{\prime}}
, hence, in particular
((r{\cdot}\gamma){\cdot}{\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho^{\prime}}})(\mathit{x})\preceq r^{\prime}
. Since
\gamma=\mathsf{G}_{\mathit{x}}
, by
Lemma

6.6
we get
\gamma{\cdot}{\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho^{\prime}}}(\mathit{x})=\mathbf{0}
, hence
(r{\cdot}\gamma){\cdot}{\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho^{\prime}}}(\mathit{x})=\mathbf{0}
as well.

With this formulation, a resource can be used only if (1) its current amount is enough to cover the required use; 2) no resource amount would be wasted.
An alternative formulation could even
remove garbage
, see
Proposition

6.16
in the following.

The side condition requiring the result to be be no-waste is also added to the other axioms
(fun)
and
(unit)
.

In rule
(pair)
, rather than be sequentially used by the two components as in the previous rule in in
Fig.

5
, the environment is split in two parts, used to evaluate the first and the second component, respectively.
A similar splitting is done, in rules for expressions, in the bottom section of
Fig.

18
, for all the expressions with more than one subterm, as mentioned above as a novelty of no-waste semantics.

Besides the standard typing errors, and resource exhaustion, as in the previous resource-aware semantics, reduction can get stuck if some available amount would be wasted. For instance, considering linearity grades, the configuration
\mathit{x}\mid\mathit{x}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}^{\emptyset}}\rangle,\mathit{y}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}^{\emptyset}}\rangle
is stuck. Indeed, rule
(var)
is not applicable since there is no way to obtain a no-waste result. Formally, since
\gamma=\emptyset
, it should be
\emptyset\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\mathit{y}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}^{\emptyset}}\rangle
; this does not hold, since, for the resource
\mathit{y}
, the usage is
0
, the availability is
1
, and
0\not\leq 1
. On the other hand, reduction would be possibile with an environment
\mathit{x}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}^{\emptyset}}\rangle,\mathit{y}:\langle{\omega},{\texttt{unit}^{\emptyset}}\rangle
, and the final result would be
\texttt{unit}^{\emptyset}\mid\mathit{y}:\langle{\omega},{\texttt{unit}^{\emptyset}}\rangle
, or even
\texttt{unit}^{\emptyset}\mid\emptyset
in a semantics removing garbage.

When the environment is split in two parts, there are generally many possible ways to do the splitting; depending on such choices, when reaching the base cases, the corresponding axiom could either be applicable or not. This is an additional source of non-determinism besides those already present in the previous semantics.
Hence, as already mentioned, soundness of the type system will be
soundness-may
11
11

11

The terminology of
may
and
must
properties is very general and comes originally from
(
DeNicolaH84
)
; the specific names soundness-may and soundness-must were introduced in
(
DagninoBZD20
;
Dagnino22
)
in the context of big-step semantics.
, meaning that, for a well-typed configuration,
there exists
a computation which does not get stuck; in particular, there exist a way to split the environment at each node such that the proof tree can be
successfully completed.

\mathit{y}\,{\!\langle^{3}\mathit{x},\mathit{x}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{5}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-6.45001pt{5}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{5}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-6.45001pt{5}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{5}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-5.75002pt{5}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{5}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-5.75002pt{5}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}}\mid\mathit{y}:\langle{1},{\texttt{f}^{\mathit{x}:2}}\rangle,\mathit{x}:\langle{10},{\texttt{u}^{\emptyset}}\rangle

3.8889pt}}}\mid\mathit{x}:\langle{12},{\texttt{u}}\rangle\Rrightarrow_{1}\langle^{3}\texttt{u},\texttt{u}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{5}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-6.24391pt{5}\kern \begin{array}[]{lc}\mathcal{D}_{2}=&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\hskip 4.62497pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 28.16803pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.68248pt,depth=-1.94249pt,width=93.33582pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(var)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{x}\mid\mathit{x}:\langle{3},{\texttt{u}^{\emptyset}}\rangle\Rrightarrow_{1}\texttt{u}^{\emptyset}\mid\mathit{x}:\langle{2},{\texttt{u}^{\emptyset}}\rangle$}}}}\hskip 4.62497pt\penalty\hskip 4.62497pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 47.78465pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.68248pt,depth=-1.94249pt,width=132.56906pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(var)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{x}\mid\mathit{x}:\langle{1},{\texttt{u}^{\emptyset}}\rangle,\mathit{x}^{\prime}:\langle{1},{\texttt{u}^{\emptyset}}\rangle\Rrightarrow_{1}\texttt{u}^{\emptyset}\mid\mathit{x}^{\prime}:\langle{1},{\texttt{u}^{\emptyset}}\rangle$}}}}\hskip 4.62497pt\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.68248pt,depth=-1.94249pt,width=287.85765pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(pair)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 26.777pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\langle^{1}\mathit{x},\mathit{x}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-6.24391pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-6.24391pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-5.6139pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-5.6139pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}\mid\mathit{x}:\langle{4},{\texttt{u}^{\emptyset}}\rangle,\mathit{x}^{\prime}:\langle{1},{\texttt{u}^{\emptyset}}\rangle\Rrightarrow_{1}\langle^{1}\texttt{u},\texttt{u}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-6.24391pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-6.24391pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-5.6139pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-5.6139pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}^{\emptyset}\mid\mathit{x}:\langle{2},{\texttt{u}^{\emptyset}}\rangle$}}}}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathcal{D}=&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 41.2999pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\hskip 4.62497pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 26.57797pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\hskip 9.24994pt$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.68248pt,depth=-1.94249pt,width=99.40564pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(var)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{y}\mid\mathit{y}:\langle{2},{\texttt{f}^{\mathit{x}:2}}\rangle\Rrightarrow_{1}\texttt{f}^{\mathit{x}:2}\mid\mathit{y}:\langle{1},{\texttt{f}}\rangle$}}}}\hskip 4.62497pt\penalty\hskip 18.49988pt\mathcal{D}_{1}\hskip 18.49988pt\mathcal{D}_{2}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.68248pt,depth=-1.94249pt,width=304.70027pt\hbox{\kern 2.77501pt$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(app)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{y}\,{\!\langle^{3}\mathit{x},\mathit{x}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{5}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-6.24391pt{5}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{5}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-6.24391pt{5}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{5}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-5.6139pt{5}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{5}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-5.6139pt{5}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}}\mid\mathit{x}:\langle{12},{\texttt{u}^{\emptyset}}\rangle,\mathit{y}:\langle{2},{\texttt{f}^{\mathit{x}:2}}\rangle\Rrightarrow_{1}\langle^{1}\texttt{u},\texttt{u}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-6.24391pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-6.24391pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-5.6139pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-5.6139pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}^{\emptyset}\mid\mathit{y}:\langle{1},{\texttt{f}}\rangle,\mathit{x}^{\prime}:\langle{1},{\texttt{u}^{\emptyset}}\rangle$}}}}\end{array}

Figure 20:
Another example of reduction

On the other hand, if we consider exact counting, the configuration is stuck. Let us consider, for instance, two ways to split
\mathit{x}:\langle{12},{\texttt{unit}^{\emptyset}}\rangle,\mathit{y}:\langle{2},{\lambda\mathit{y}.\langle^{1}\mathit{x},\mathit{x}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-6.45001pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-6.45001pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-5.75002pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-5.75002pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}^{\mathit{x}:2}}\rangle
in rule
(app)
in
\mathcal{D}
. The former, as in
Fig.

20
, is to allocate both the copies of
\mathit{y}
to reduce the function. In this case, the computation is stuck when we try to apply rule
(var)
in
\mathcal{D}
, since
1\not\leq 2
, that is, we cannot consume only one copy of
\mathit{y}
since the remaining grade would be non-discardable. The latter is to allocate one copy of
\mathit{y}
to reduce the function and one to reduce its argument. In this case the computation is also stuck, when we try to apply rule
(var)
in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
; indeed, here
\mathit{y}
is not used and its grade is not discardable, that is, we would waste
\mathit{y}
.

6.5
Results

As expected, no-waste semantics can be seen as a refinement of resource-aware semantics.
More precisely, given a no-waste reduction, then there exist infinitely many resource-aware reductions, each one essentially adding a “wasted” portion of environment. This is formalized below, where, if
\mathcal{V}=\langle{\gamma},{\mathbf{v}}\rangle
, then
\mathsf{erase}(\mathcal{V})
denotes
\mathbf{v}
, and analogously
\mathsf{erase}(\rho)
denotes the environment obtained from
\rho
by removing annotations from values.

Theorem 6.12
.

If
\mathit{E}\mid\rho_{1}\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}^{\gamma}\mid\rho_{2}
then

\mathit{E}\mid\mathsf{erase}(\rho)+\hat{\rho}\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}\mid\mathsf{erase}(\rho^{\prime})+\hat{\rho}
.

Proof.

Let us write
\rho\|\rho^{\prime}
if
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
are non-conflicting, that is,
\rho+\rho^{\prime}
is defined. First of all note that, if
\mathit{E}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}^{\gamma}\mid\rho^{\prime}
, and
\mathsf{erase}(\rho)\|\hat{\rho}
holds, then
\mathsf{erase}(\rho^{\prime})\|\hat{\rho}
holds as well, since variables added in
\rho^{\prime}
are fresh.

By induction on the no-waste reduction. We show one base and one inductive case.

(var)

We have
\mathit{x}\mid\rho,\mathit{x}:\langle{s},{\mathcal{V}}\rangle\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho,\mathit{x}:\langle{s^{\prime}},{\mathcal{V}}\rangle
, with
\mathcal{V}=\mathbf{v}^{\gamma}
and
r+s^{\prime}\preceq s
. Take an arbitrary
\hat{\rho}
such that

\mathsf{erase}(\rho,\mathit{x}:\langle{s},{\mathcal{V}}\rangle)\|\hat{\rho}
.

If
\mathit{x}\not\in\mathsf{dom}(\hat{\rho})
, then the side condition
r+s^{\prime}\preceq s
of rule
(var)
in
Fig.

5
holds, hence
\mathit{x}\mid(\mathsf{erase}(\rho),\mathit{x}:\langle{s},{\mathbf{v}}\rangle)+\hat{\rho}\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}\mid\mathsf{erase}(\rho),\mathit{x}:\langle{s^{\prime}},{\mathbf{v}}\rangle+\hat{\rho}
.

If
{\hat{\rho}}(\mathit{x})=\langle{r^{\prime}},{\mathbf{v}}\rangle
, then the side condition
r+s^{\prime}+r^{\prime}\preceq s+r^{\prime}
of rule
(var)
in
Fig.

5
holds, hence
\mathit{x}\mid(\mathsf{erase}(\rho),\mathit{x}:\langle{s},{\mathbf{v}}\rangle)+\hat{\rho}\Rightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}\mid\mathsf{erase}(\rho),\mathit{x}:\langle{s^{\prime}},{\mathbf{v}}\rangle+\hat{\rho}
.
In both cases we get the thesis.

(pair)

We have

(1)
\mathit{v}_{1}\mid\rho_{1}\Rrightarrow_{r{\cdot}r_{1}}\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}\mid\rho_{1}^{\prime}

(2)
\mathit{v}_{2}\mid\rho_{2}\Rrightarrow_{r{\cdot}r_{2}}\mathbf{v}_{2}^{\gamma_{2}}\mid\rho_{2}^{\prime}

(3)
{\langle^{r_{1}}\mathit{v}_{1},\mathit{v}_{2}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\langle^{r_{1}}\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}^{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}}\mid\rho_{1}^{\prime}+\rho_{2}^{\prime}}

(4)
\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}\preceq\rho

From (4) we get that
\mathsf{erase}(\rho_{1})\|\mathsf{erase}(\rho_{2})
holds.
Hence, from (1) by inductive hypothesis, taking as additional environment
\mathsf{erase}(\rho_{2})
, we get

\mathit{v}_{1}\mid\mathsf{erase}(\rho_{1})+\mathsf{erase}(\rho_{2})\Rrightarrow_{r{\cdot}r_{1}}\mathbf{v}_{1}\mid\mathsf{erase}(\rho^{\prime}_{1})+\mathsf{erase}(\rho_{2})

Since
\mathsf{erase}(\rho^{\prime}_{1})\|\mathsf{erase}(\rho_{2})
,
from (2) by inductive hypothesis, taking as additional environment
\mathsf{erase}(\rho^{\prime}_{1})
, we get

\mathit{v}_{2}\mid\mathsf{erase}(\rho_{2})+\mathsf{erase}(\rho^{\prime}_{1})\Rrightarrow_{r{\cdot}r_{1}}\mathbf{v}_{1}\mid\mathsf{erase}(\rho^{\prime}_{2})+\mathsf{erase}(\rho^{\prime}_{1})

Hence, we can apply rule
(pair)
in
Fig.

5
:

{\langle^{r_{1}}\mathit{v}_{1},\mathit{v}_{2}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{r}\langle^{r_{1}}\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}\mid\mathsf{erase}(\rho_{1}^{\prime})+\mathsf{erase}(\rho_{2}^{\prime})}

and we get the thesis.

∎

We state now the main result of this section: the reduction relation is no-waste.
We say that
\mathit{E}\mid\rho
is no-waste, written
\vdash_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\mathit{E}\mid\rho
, if
\gamma\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\rho
holds for some
\gamma
honest for
\mathit{E}
.

Theorem 6.13

(Semantics is no-waste)
.

If
\mathit{E}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}^{\gamma^{\prime}}\mid\rho^{\prime}
then
\vdash_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\mathit{E}\mid\rho
, and
r{\cdot}\gamma^{\prime}\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\rho^{\prime}
.

Here,
r{\cdot}\gamma^{\prime}\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\rho^{\prime}
states that, as required,
only no-waste results are produced
; since the final result is produced in
r
copies, the no-waste condition holds for a grade context obtained by multiplying by
r
that declared in the annotated value.

In addition, the theorem provides a
characterization of configurations which do not get stuck due to wasting errors
. In other words, configurations such that
\vdash_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\mathit{E}\mid\rho
does not hold are stuck, since they cannot be reduced to a no-waste result.

Let us consider the configuration

\mathit{x}\mid\mathit{x}:\langle{1},{(\lambda\_.\langle^{1}\mathit{y},\mathit{z}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-6.45001pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-6.45001pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-5.75002pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{1}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-5.75002pt{1}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}})^{\mathit{y}:1,\mathit{z}:1},\mathit{y}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}^{\emptyset}}\rangle,\mathit{z}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}^{\emptyset}}\rangle}\rangle
.

This configuration is non-stuck, since, assigning grade
1
to
\mathit{x}
, the program uses with no-waste the environment.
On the contrary, the configuration
\mathit{x}\mid\mathit{x}:\langle{1},{(\lambda\_.\mathit{y})^{\mathit{y}:1},\mathit{y}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}^{\emptyset}}\rangle,\mathit{z}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}^{\emptyset}}\rangle}\rangle
is stuck, since
there is no way
to assign grades to variables so that the no-waste condition holds. Indeed, we should assign
1
to
\mathit{z}
, but this would not be honest for the value expression
\mathit{x}
.

Of course we expect, and will prove, that well-typed configurations are no-waste; however, note that the characterization above is purely semantic, and, as usual, it can hold for ill-typed configurations. For instance, assuming to have booleans and conditional, which can be encoded as shown in
Fig.

11
, the configuration

\texttt{if}\ \texttt{true}\ \texttt{then}\ \mathit{x}\ \texttt{else}\ \texttt{unit}\mid\mathit{x}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}^{\emptyset}}\rangle
is no-waste, since
\mathit{x}:1\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\mathit{x}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle
holds, and indeed can be safely reduced to
\texttt{unit}^{\emptyset}\mid\emptyset
. However, this configuration is ill-typed.

Theorem

6.13
is an immediate corollary of the following one.

Theorem 6.14

(Resource balance)
.

If
\mathit{E}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}^{\gamma^{\prime}}\mid\rho^{\prime}
then there exist
\gamma,\gamma^{c},\gamma^{a}
, with
\gamma
honest for
\mathit{E}
,
\gamma(\mathit{x})=\mathbf{0}
for each
\mathit{x}\not\in\mathsf{dom}(\rho)
,
and
\gamma^{a}(\mathit{x})=\mathbf{0}
for all
\mathit{x}\in\mathsf{dom}(\rho)
, such that
the following conditions hold.

1.

r{\cdot}\gamma^{\prime}{\cdot}\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho^{\prime}}\preceq\gamma_{\rho^{\prime}}

2.

\gamma{\cdot}\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho}\preceq\gamma_{\rho}

3.

\gamma_{\rho^{\prime}}+\gamma^{c}\preceq\gamma_{\rho}+\gamma^{a}

4.

\gamma{\cdot}\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho}+\gamma^{a}\preceq r{\cdot}\gamma^{\prime}{\cdot}\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho^{\prime}}+\gamma^{c}

Notably,
Item

1
shows that the result is no-waste, and

\gamma
in
Item

2
is the grade context
used to prove that the configuration is no-waste. The grade contexts

\gamma^{c}
and
\gamma^{a}
denote, respectively,
consumed
and
added
resources during the computation, which are used to express balance (in)equations in
Items

3
and
4
. Added resources are always fresh, hence
\gamma^{a}(\mathit{x})=\mathbf{0}
for all
\mathit{x}\in\mathsf{dom}(\rho)
is expected to hold.
The inequation of
Item

3
states that remaining resources plus those consumed during the computation cannot exceed the sum of initially available resources and those added by the evaluation.
This is analogous to a resource-balance result proved in
(
ChoudhuryEEW21
)
, but here it is expressed in purely semantic terms.

Item

4
provides a strict version formulated using the more precise information carried by the grade graphs of the environments and is the key property to prove the no-waste result.

The no-waste property implies, in particular, that any resource in the environment which is not reachable from the program (garbage) is discardable, as formalized below. As mentioned before, such resource could be actually discarded in a semantics removing garbage.

Let us denote by
\xrightarrow{\star}_{\rho}
the
reachability relation
in
\rho
, that is, the reflexive and transitive closure of the relation
\rightarrow_{\rho}
defined by:

\mathit{x}\rightarrow_{\rho}\mathit{y}
if
\rho(\mathit{x})=\langle{\_},{\mathbf{v}^{\_}}\rangle
and
\mathit{y}\in\mathsf{fv}(\mathbf{v})

The following lemma states that, as expected, the existence a non-discardable path implies reachability.

Lemma 6.15
.

If
\mathbf{0}\not\preceq\mathsf{G}_{\rho}(\mathit{p})
, then
\mathsf{in}(\mathit{p})\xrightarrow{\star}_{\rho}\mathsf{out}(\mathit{p})
.

Proof.

By induction on the length of
\mathit{p}
.
If
\mathit{p}=\mathit{x}
, then
\mathit{x}\xrightarrow{\star}_{\rho}\mathit{x}
by reflexivity. Otherwise,
\mathit{p}=\mathit{x}\cdot\mathit{p}^{\prime}
, hence
\rho(\mathit{x})=\langle{\_},{\mathbf{v}^{\gamma}}\rangle
. From
\mathbf{0}\not\preceq\mathsf{G}_{\rho}(\mathit{x}\cdot\mathit{p})
we get

\mathbf{0}\not\preceq\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x},\mathsf{in}(\mathit{p}^{\prime}))
and
\mathbf{0}\not\preceq\mathsf{G}_{\rho}(\mathit{p}^{\prime})
.
Being
\gamma
honest for
\mathbf{v}
, we get
\mathsf{in}(\mathit{p}^{\prime})\in\mathsf{fv}(\mathbf{v})
,
hence
\mathit{x}\rightarrow_{\rho}\mathsf{in}(\mathit{p}^{\prime})
, and we get the thesis by applying the inductive hypothesis to
\mathbf{0}\not\preceq\mathsf{G}_{\rho}(\mathit{p}^{\prime})
, and then by transitivity.

∎

We write
X\xrightarrow{\star}_{\rho}\mathit{y}
if
\mathit{x}\xrightarrow{\star}_{\rho}\mathit{y}
holds for some
\mathit{x}\in X
.

Proposition 6.16

(No-waste
\Rightarrow
Garbage is Discardable)
.

If
\mathit{E}\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\rho
holds:

\mathsf{fv}(\mathit{E})\not\xrightarrow{\star}_{\rho}\mathit{x}
implies
\mathbf{0}\preceq\gamma_{\rho}(\mathit{x})
.

Proof.

Set
r=\gamma_{\rho}(\mathit{x})
.
We prove that
\mathbf{0}\not\preceq r
implies
\mathsf{fv}(\mathit{E})\xrightarrow{\star}_{\rho}\mathit{x}
.

For some
\gamma
honest for
\mathit{E}
, we have
\gamma{\cdot}{\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho}}(\mathit{x})\preceq r
. Since
\mathbf{0}\not\preceq r
, it is necessarily
\mathbf{0}\not\preceq\gamma{\cdot}{\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho}}(\mathit{x})
, that is,
\mathbf{0}\not\preceq\sum_{\mathit{y}\in\mathsf{V}}\gamma(\mathit{y})\cdot\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho}(\mathit{y},\mathit{x})
. Hence
\mathbf{0}\not\preceq\gamma(\mathit{y})\cdot\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho}(\mathit{y},\mathit{x})
for some
\mathit{y}
, and this implies
\mathbf{0}\not\preceq\gamma(\mathit{y})
and
\mathbf{0}\not\preceq\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho}(\mathit{y},\mathit{x})
, hence there is a path
\mathit{p}
from
\mathit{y}
to
\mathit{x}
with
\mathbf{0}\not\preceq\mathsf{G}_{\rho}(\mathit{p})
.
Being
\gamma
honest for
\mathit{E}
, we get
\mathit{y}\in\mathsf{fv}(\mathit{E})
, hence
\mathit{x}\rightarrow_{\rho}\mathit{y}
, and by
Lemma

6.15
and transitivity we get the thesis.
∎

Let us consider the type system in
Section

4
, with the only difference that, as shown in
Fig.

21
, annotations in values are taken into account. That is, it is checked in rule
(t-val)
, that they are (overapproximated by) the direct usage obtained by typechecking the value. Moreover, since annotated values are not value expressions, we have to add a typing rule for results.

\begin{array}[]{l}\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-val)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{v}:{\tau}^{\mathbf{1}}}{r{\cdot}\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{v}^{\gamma}:{\tau}^{r}}\begin{array}[]{l}\gamma\preceq\gamma_{\Gamma}\end{array}\par\\[17.22217pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-env)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Gamma_{i}\vdash\mathcal{V}_{i}:\tau_{i}^{r_{i}}\quad\forall i\in 1..n}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{x}_{1}:\langle{r_{1}},{\mathcal{V}_{1}}\rangle,\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}:\langle{r_{n}},{\mathcal{V}_{n}}\rangle\triangleright\Delta}\begin{array}[]{l}\Gamma=\mathit{x}_{1}:_{r_{1}}\tau_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}:_{r_{n}}\tau_{n}\\
\Gamma_{1}+\ldots+\Gamma_{n}+\Delta\preceq\Gamma\end{array}\par\\[17.22217pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-conf)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Delta\vdash\mathit{E}:\mathit{T}\qquad\Gamma\vdash\rho\triangleright\Delta}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{E}\mid\rho:\mathit{T}}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\qquad\scriptstyle{\textsc{(t-res)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\Delta\vdash\mathcal{V}:\mathit{T}\qquad\Gamma\vdash\rho\triangleright\Delta}{\Gamma\vdash\mathcal{V}\mid\rho:\mathit{T}}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}\par\par\end{array}

Figure 21:
Typing rules for (annotated) values, environments, configurations, and results

We can prove that well-typed configurations and results are no-waste (
Theorem

6.20
).
The proof relies on the induction principle of
\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho}
described in
Proposition

6.10
.
The next proposition shows that well-typed environments always satisfy the hypothesis of such induction principle.

Proposition 6.17
.

If
\Gamma\vdash\rho\triangleright\Delta
then
\gamma_{\Delta}+\gamma_{\rho}{\cdot}\mathsf{G}_{\rho}\preceq\gamma_{\rho}
.

Proposition 6.18
.

If
\Gamma\vdash\rho\triangleright\Delta
then
\gamma_{\Delta}{\cdot}\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho}\preceq\gamma_{\rho}
.

Proof.

It is an immediate consequence of
Propositions

6.10
and
6.17
.
∎

The above proposition guarantees that a well-typed environment is non-waste with respect to the grade contxt extracted from the residual typing context.
The next lemma shows that the grade context extracted from the typing context of a well-typed expression is always honest for it.

Lemma 6.19
.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{E}:\mathit{T}
and
\mathbf{0}\not\preceq\gamma_{\Gamma}(\mathit{x})
then
\mathit{x}\in\mathsf{fv}(\mathit{E})
.

Proof.

By induction on the typing rules.
∎

Theorem 6.20

(Well-typedness implies no-waste)
.

1.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{E}\mid\rho:\mathit{T}
then
\vdash_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\mathit{E}\mid\rho
.

2.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{v}^{\gamma}\mid\rho:{\tau}^{r}
then
r{\cdot}\gamma\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\rho
.

Proof.

1.

We have to show that
\gamma\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\rho
for some
\gamma
honest for
\mathit{E}
.
To derive
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{E}\mid\rho:\mathit{T}
,
we have necessarily applied rule
(t-conf)
, hence
\Delta\vdash\mathit{E}:\mathit{T}
and
\Gamma\vdash\rho\triangleright\Delta
hold for some
\Delta
. By
Proposition

6.18
we have
\gamma_{\Delta}{\cdot}\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho}\preceq\gamma_{\rho}
, that is,
\gamma_{\Delta}\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\rho
. Moreover,
\gamma_{\Delta}
is honest for
\mathit{E}
by
Lemma

6.19
.

2.

To derive
\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{v}^{\gamma}\mid\rho:{\tau}^{r}
,
we have necessarily applied rule
(t-res)
, hence
\Delta\vdash\mathbf{v}^{\gamma}:{\tau}^{r}
and
\Gamma\vdash\rho\triangleright\Delta
hold for some
\Delta
.
Moreover, to derive
\Delta\vdash\mathbf{v}^{\gamma}:{\tau}^{r}
we have necessarily applied rule
(t-val)
, hence
\Delta=r{\cdot}\Delta^{\prime}
and
\gamma\preceq\gamma_{\Delta^{\prime}}
. Moreover, by definition
\gamma_{\Delta}=r{\cdot}\gamma_{\Delta^{\prime}}
, hence
r{\cdot}\gamma\preceq\gamma_{\Delta}
. By
Proposition

6.18
we have
\gamma_{\Delta}{\cdot}\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho}\preceq\gamma_{\rho}
, hence
(r{\cdot}\gamma){\cdot}\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho}\preceq\gamma_{\rho}
, that is,
r{\cdot}\gamma\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\rho
.

∎

We end this section by briefly discussing the acyclicity assumption on enviroments. Technically, this allows us to give
Definition

6.5
, where an infinite sum of path grades can be reduced to a finite one thanks to
Proposition

6.4
(
4
).
However, infinite sums would be well-defined as well by assuming suitable conditions on the grade algebra
(
Golan03
;
EsikK04
;
EsikL05
;
FengJ09
)
.
A more serious problem is that the proof of
Proposition

6.10
relies on acyclicity; that is, there is no guarantee that the condition ensured by well-typedness (
\gamma_{\Delta}+\gamma_{\rho}{\cdot}\mathsf{G}_{\rho}\preceq\gamma_{\rho}
) actually implies the no-waste condition (
\gamma_{\Delta}{\cdot}\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho}\preceq\gamma_{\rho}
). In a sense, the meaning of no-waste in presence of cyclic environments is unclear. Consider a cyclic resource which is “garbage”, that is, not reachable from the program, as in, e.g.,
\mathbf{v}\mid\mathit{x}:\langle{r},{\lambda z.\mathit{x}^{\mathit{x}:1}}\rangle
, with
\mathbf{v}
closed. This configuration would be well-typed for an arbitrary grade
r
, since the resource is “auto-consuming”. We will investigate meaning and formalization of no-waste for cyclic resources in future work.

7
Type soundness

In this section, we prove our main result: soundness of the type system with respect to the no-waste semantics
12
12

12

Thanks to
Theorem

6.12
, this implies soundness with respect to the semantics in
Fig.

5
as well.
.
That is, for well-typed expressions there is a computation which is not stuck for any reason, including resource exhaustion and waste.
Note that this is a
may
flavour of soundness
(
DeNicolaH84
;
DagninoBZD20
;
Dagnino22
)
, which is the only one we can prove in this context, because the semantics is non-deterministic.
We analyse separately type soundness for value expressions and expressions.
In the following, we will say that two judgements

\Gamma_{1}\vdash\rho_{1}\triangleright\Delta_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}\vdash\rho_{2}\triangleright\Delta_{2}
are
consistent
if
in their derivations
the values associated with variables in the common domain of
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
are typed with the same type and typing context.
Formally, this means that
for all
\mathit{x}\in\mathsf{dom}(\rho_{1})\cap\mathsf{dom}(\rho_{2})
, there are
\mathcal{V}=\mathbf{v}^{\gamma}
,
\tau
and
\Delta
such that

\rho_{1}(\mathit{x})=\langle{\_},{\mathcal{V}}\rangle
,
\rho_{2}(\mathit{x})=\langle{\_},{\mathcal{V}}\rangle
,

\Gamma_{1}(\mathit{x})=\langle{\_},{\tau}\rangle
,
\Gamma_{2}(\mathit{x})=\langle{\_},{\tau}\rangle
, and
in the derivations of both judgements there is a premise

\Delta\vdash\mathbf{v}:{\tau}^{\mathbf{1}}
.
Similarly, we say that two typing judgements for configurations are consistent if so are the judgements of the environments they contain.

Type soundness for value expressions

Since reduction of value expressions cannot diverge, type soundness means that, if well-typed, then they reduce to a value,
as stated below.

Theorem 7.1

(Soundness for value expressions)
.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}\mid\rho:{\tau}^{r}
, then
\mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho^{\prime}
for some
\mathcal{V}
,
\rho^{\prime}
.

This is a corollary of the following extended formulation which can be proved inductively on the structure of value expressions, relying on standard lemmas.

Theorem 7.2

(Soundness for value expressions (extended))
.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}\mid\rho:{\tau}^{r}
then

\mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho^{\prime}
and

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathcal{V}\mid\rho^{\prime}:{\tau}^{r}

consistent with
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}\mid\rho:{\tau}^{r}
, for some
\mathcal{V}
,
\rho^{\prime}
,
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Note that, even though reduction of value expressions just performs substitution, in a resource-aware semantics this is a significant event, since it implies consuming some amount of resources.
Theorem

7.1
states that no resource exhaustion or waste can happen.

Adding divergence

For expressions, instead, big-step semantics, as those defined in
Fig.

5
and
Fig.

18
, suffer from the long-known drawback
(
CousotC92
;
LeroyG09
)
that non-terminating and stuck computations are indistinguishable, since in both cases no finite proof tree of a judgment can be constructed.
This is an issue for our aim: to prove that for a well-typed expression there is a computation which does not get stuck, that is, either produces a value or diverges.
To solve this problem, we extend the big-step semantics to explicitly model diverging computations, proceeding as follows, with
\mathit{c}::=\mathit{e}\mid\rho
.

•

the shape of the judgment for expressions is generalized to
\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\textsc{r}
, where the
result

r
is either a pair consisting of a value and a final environment, or
divergence
(
\infty
);

•

this judgment is defined through a
generalized inference system
, shown in
Fig.

22
, consisting of the
rules
from
(ret)
to
(match-u/match-u-div)
, and the
corule

(co-div)

(differences with respect to the previous semantics in the bottom section of
Fig.

18
are emphasized in grey
13
13

13

Recall that, since the evaluation judgment is stratified, premises involving the judgment for value expressions can be equivalently considered as side conditions.
).

The key point here is that, in generalized inference systems,
rules are interpreted in an
essentially coinductive
, rather than inductive, way.
For details on generalized inference systems
we refer to
(
AnconaDZ@esop17
;
Dagnino19
)
;
here, for the reader’s convenience, we provide a self-contained presentation, instantiating general definitions on our specific case.

\begin{array}[]{lcll}\textsc{r}&::=&\mathcal{V}\mid\rho&\mbox{result}\\
&\mid&\infty&\mbox{}\\[7.74997pt]
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{l}\\
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(ret)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rightarrow_{s}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho^{\prime}}{\texttt{return}\ \mathit{v}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho^{\prime}}\begin{array}[]{l}r\preceq s\neq\mathbf{0}\end{array}\hskip 18.49988pt\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(let-div1)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\mathit{e}_{1}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{s}\infty}{\texttt{let}\ \mathit{x}=\mathit{e}_{1}\ \texttt{in}\ \mathit{e}_{2}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\infty}\begin{array}[]{l}\end{array}$}\\[15.49994pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(let/let-div2)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{e}_{1}\mid\rho_{1}\Rrightarrow_{s}\mathcal{V}_{1}\mid\rho_{1}^{\prime}\\

\mathit{e}[\mathit{x}^{\prime}/\mathit{x}][\mathit{f}^{\prime}/\mathit{f}]\mid(\rho_{1}^{\prime}+\rho_{2}^{\prime}),\mathit{x}^{\prime}:\langle{t},{\mathcal{V}_{2}}\rangle,\mathit{f}^{\prime}:\langle{s_{2}},{\texttt{rec}\,\mathit{f}.\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}^{\gamma_{1}}}\rangle\Rrightarrow_{r}\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\textsc{r}$}\end{array}}{\mathit{v}_{1}\,{\!\mathit{v}_{2}}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\textsc{r}$}}\begin{array}[]{l}s_{1}+s_{2}\preceq s\\

\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}\preceq\rho\\
\mathit{f}^{\prime},\mathit{x}^{\prime}\ \mbox{fresh}\end{array}\par\\[15.49994pt]

\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}\preceq\rho\end{array}\par\\[15.49994pt]
\scriptstyle{\textsc{(match-p/match-p-div)}}\ \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{v}\mid\rho_{1}\Rrightarrow_{s}\langle^{r_{1}}\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.22601pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.22601pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.62254pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.62254pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.53891pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}^{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}}\mid\rho_{1}^{\prime}\\
\mathit{e}[\mathit{x}^{\prime}/\mathit{x}][\mathit{y}^{\prime}/\mathit{y}]\mid\rho,\mathit{x}^{\prime}:\langle{s{\cdot}r_{1}},{\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}}\rangle,\mathit{y}^{\prime}:\langle{s{\cdot}r_{2}},{\mathbf{v}_{2}^{\gamma_{2}}}\rangle\Rrightarrow_{r}\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\textsc{r}$}\end{array}}{\texttt{match}\ \mathit{v}\ \texttt{with}\ \langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle\rightarrow\mathit{e}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$\textsc{r}$}}\begin{array}[]{l}s\neq\mathbf{0}\\

Figure 22:
Adding divergence

Rules in
Fig.

22
handle divergence propagation. Notably, for each rule in the bottom section of
Fig.

18
, we add a divergence propagation rule for each of the possibly diverging premises.
The only rule with two possibly diverging premises is
(let)
. Hence, divergence propagation for an expression
\texttt{let}\ \mathit{x}=\mathit{e}_{1}\ \texttt{in}\ \mathit{e}_{2}
is obtained by two (meta)rules:

(let-div1)

when
\mathit{e}_{1}
diverges, and
(let-div2)
when
\mathit{e}_{1}
converges and
\mathit{e}_{2}
diverges; in
Fig.

22
, for brevity, this second metarule is merged with
(let)
, using the metavariable
r
.
All the other rules have only one possibly diverging premise, so one divergence propagation rule is added and merged with the original metarule, analogously to
(let-div2)
.

In generalized inference systems, infinite proof trees are allowed.
Hence, judgments
\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\infty
can be derived, as desired, even though there is no axiom introducing them,
thus distinguishing diverging computations (infinite proof tree) from stuck computations (no proof tree).
However, a purely coinductive interpretation would allow the derivation of spurious judgements
(
CousotC92
;
LeroyG09
;
AnconaDZ17
)
.
To address this issue, generalized inference systems may include
corules
, written with a thick line, only
(co-div)
in our case,
which refine the coinductive interpretation, filtering out some (undesired) infinite derivations.
Intuitively, the meaning of
(co-div)
is to allow infinite derivations only for divergence (see
Example

7.4
below).
Formally, we have the following definition instantiated from
(
AnconaDZ@esop17
;
Dagnino19
)
.

Definition 7.3
.

A judgment
\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\textsc{r}
is derivable in the generalized inference system in
Fig.

22
, written
\vdash_{\infty}\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\textsc{r}
, if it has an infinite proof tree constructed using the rules where, moreover, each node has a
finite
proof tree constructed using the rules
plus the corule
.

Example 7.4
.

Let us consider again the expression
\mathit{y}\texttt{;}\mathit{f}\,{\!\mathit{x}}
of
Example

3.3
.
Now, its non-terminating evaluation in the environment
\mathit{y}:\langle{\infty},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle,\mathit{f}:\langle{\infty},{\mathsf{div}}\rangle,\mathit{x}:\langle{1},{\texttt{unit}}\rangle
, abbreviated
\langle{\infty}{,}{\infty}{,}{1}\rangle
using the previous convention, is formalized by the infinite proof tree in
Fig.

23
, where instantiations of (meta)rules
(match-u)
and
(app)
have been replaced by those of the corresponding divergence propagation rule.
It is immediate to see that each node in such infinite proof tree has a finite proof tree constructed using the rules plus the corule:
the only nodes which have no finite proof tree constructed using the rules are those in the infinite path, of shape either
\mathit{y}\texttt{;}\mathit{f}\,{\!\mathit{x}}\mid\langle{\infty}{,}{\infty}{,}{1}\rangle\Rrightarrow\infty
or
\mathit{f}\,{\!\mathit{x}}\mid\langle{\infty}{,}{\infty}{,}{1}\rangle\Rrightarrow\infty
, and such judgments are directly derivable by the corule.
On the other hand, infinite proof trees obtained by
using
(match-u)
and
(app)
would derive
\mathit{y}\texttt{;}\mathit{f}\,{\!\mathit{x}}\mid\langle{\infty}{,}{\infty}{,}{1}\rangle\Rrightarrow\mathcal{V}\mid\langle{\infty}{,}{\infty}{,}{1}\rangle
for any
\mathcal{V}
.
However,
such judgments
have no
finite proof tree using also the corule, which allows only to introduce divergence, since there is no rule deriving a value with divergence as a premise.

\begin{array}[]{l}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\hskip 4.09723pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 42.01262pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.37639pt,depth=-1.72084pt,width=84.02524pt\hbox{\kern 2.3917pt$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(var)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{y}\mid\langle{\infty}{,}{\infty}{,}{1}\rangle\Rrightarrow\texttt{u}\mid\langle{\infty}{,}{\infty}{,}{1}\rangle$}}}}\hskip 4.09723pt\penalty\!\!\!\!\hskip 4.09723pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\hskip 4.09723pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 44.42097pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.37639pt,depth=-1.72084pt,width=88.84195pt\hbox{\kern 2.3917pt$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(var)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{f}\mid\langle{\infty}{,}{\infty}{,}{1}\rangle\Rrightarrow_{\infty}\mathsf{div}\mid\langle{\infty}{,}{0}{,}{1}\rangle$}}}}\hskip 4.09723pt\penalty\!\!\!\!\!\!\hskip 4.09723pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 37.92374pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule 4.09723pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 29.49101pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\ldots$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule
