0.0pt\vrule height=2.37639pt,depth=-1.72084pt,width=292.54053pt\hbox{\kern 2.3917pt$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(app-div)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 116.87508pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{f}\,{\!\mathit{x}}\mid\langle{\infty}{,}{\infty}{,}{1}\rangle\Rrightarrow\infty$}}}}\hskip 4.09723pt\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.37639pt,depth=-1.72084pt,width=418.84058pt\hbox{\kern 2.3917pt$\scriptstyle{\textsc{(match-u-div)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 176.17928pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathit{y}\texttt{;}\mathit{f}\,{\!\mathit{x}}\mid\langle{\infty}{,}{\infty}{,}{1}\rangle\Rrightarrow\infty$}}}}\end{array}

Figure 23:
Example of resource-aware evaluation: divergency with no exhaustion

The transformation from the inductive big-step semantics in
Fig.

18
to that handling divergence in
Fig.

22
is an instance of a general construction, taking as input an arbitrary big-step semantics, fully formalized, and proved to be correct, in
(
Dagnino22
)
. In particular, the construction is
conservative
, that is, the semantics of converging computations is not affected, as stated in the following result, which is an instance of Theorem 6.3 in
(
Dagnino22
)
.

Definition 7.5
.

Let
\vdash\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho
denote that the judgment can be derived by the rules in the bottom section of
Fig.

18
, interpreted inductively.

Theorem 7.6

(Conservativity)
.

\vdash_{\infty}\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho^{\prime}
if and only if
{\vdash\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho^{\prime}}
.

Note that to achieve this result corules are essential since, as observed in
Example

7.4
,
a purely coinductive interpretation allows for infinite proof trees deriving values.

Type soundness for expressions

The definition of the semantics by the generalized inference system in
Fig.

22
allows a very simple and clean formulation of type soundness: well-typed configurations always reduce to a result (which can be possibly divergence).
Formally:

Theorem 7.7

(Soundness)
.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{c}:{\tau}^{r}
, then
\vdash_{\infty}\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\textsc{r}
for some
r
.

We describe now the structure of the proof, which is interesting in itself; indeed, the semantics being big-step, there is no consolidated proof technique as the long-time known progress plus subject reduction for the small-step case
(
WrightF94
)
.

The proof is driven by coinductive reasoning on the semantic rules, following a schema firstly adopted in
(
AnconaDZ17
)
, as detailed below.
First of all, to the aim of such proof, it is convenient to turn to the following equivalent formulation of type soundness, stating that well-typed configurations which do not converge necessarily diverge.

Theorem 7.8

(Completeness-
\infty
)
.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{c}:{\tau}^{r}
, and there is no
\mathcal{V}\mid\rho
s.t.
\vdash_{\infty}\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho
,
then
\vdash_{\infty}\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\infty
.

Indeed, with this formulation soundness of the type system can be seen as
completeness
of the set of judgements
\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\infty
which are derivable with respect to the set of pairs
\langle{\mathit{c}},{r}\rangle
such that
\mathit{c}
is well-typed with grade
r
, and does not converge. The standard technique to prove completeness of a coinductive definition with respect to a specification
S
is the coinduction principle, that is, by showing that
S
is
consistent
with respect to the coinductive definition. This means that each element of
S
should be the consequence of a rule whose premises are in
S
as well. In our case, since the definition of
\vdash_{\infty}\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\infty
is not purely coinductive, but refined by the corule, completeness needs to be proved by the
bounded coinduction
principle
(
AnconaDZ@esop17
;
Dagnino19
)
, a generalization of the coinduction principle. Namely, besides proving that
S
is consistent, we have to prove that
S
is
bounded
, that is, each element of
S
can be derived by the inference system consisting of the rules
and the corules
, in our case, only
(co-div)
, interpreted inductively.

The proof of
Theorem

7.8
modularly relies on two results. The former (
Theorem

7.9
) is the instantiation of a general result proved in
(
AnconaDZ17
)
(Theorem 3.3) by bounded coinduction. For the reader’s convenience, to illustrate the proof technique in a self-contained way, we report here statement and proof for our specific case.
Namely,
Theorem

7.9
states completeness of diverging configurations with respect to any family of configurations which satisfies the
progress-
\infty
property
(this name is chosen to suggest that, for a non-converging well-typed configuration, the construction of a proof tree can never get stuck). The latter (
Theorem

7.10
) is the progress-
\infty
property for our type system.

Theorem 7.9

(Progress-
\infty\Rightarrow
Completeness-
\infty
)
.

For each grade
r
, let
\mathit{C}_{r}
be a set of configurations, and set
\mathit{C}_{r}^{\infty}=\{\mathit{c}\in\mathit{C}_{r}\mid\not\exists\ \mathcal{V}\mid\rho\ \mbox{such that}\ \vdash\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho\}
.
If the following condition holds:
14
14

14

Keep in mind that
\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\textsc{r}
denotes just the judgment (a triple), whereas
\vdash\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\textsc{r}
and
\vdash_{\infty}\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\textsc{r}
denote derivability of the judgment (
Definition

7.5
and
Definition

7.3
, respectively).

(progress-
\infty
)

\mathit{c}\in\mathit{C}_{r}^{\infty}
implies that
\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\infty
is the consequence of a rule where, for all premises of shape
\mathit{c}^{\prime}\Rrightarrow_{s}\infty
,
\mathit{c}^{\prime}\in\mathit{C}^{\infty}_{s}
, and, for all premises of shape
\mathit{c}^{\prime}\Rrightarrow_{s}\textsc{r}
,
with
\textsc{r}\neq\infty
,
\vdash\mathit{c}^{\prime}\Rrightarrow_{s}\textsc{r}
.

then
\mathit{c}\in\mathit{C}^{\infty}_{r}
implies
\vdash_{\infty}\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\infty
.

Proof.

We set
S=\{\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\infty\mid\mathit{c}\in\mathit{C}^{\infty}_{r}\}\cup\{\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\textsc{r}\mid\textsc{r}\neq\infty,\vdash\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\textsc{r}\}
, and prove that, for each
\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\textsc{r}\in S
, we have
\vdash_{\infty}\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\textsc{r}
, by bounded coinduction. We have to prove
two conditions.

1.

S
is consistent, that is, each
\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\textsc{r}
in
S
is the consequence of a rule whose premises are in
S
as well. We reason by cases:

•

For each
\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\infty\in S
, by the
(progress-
\infty
)
hypothesis it is the consequence of a rule where, for all premises of shape
\mathit{c}^{\prime}\Rrightarrow_{s}\infty
,
\mathit{c}^{\prime}\in\mathit{C}^{\infty}_{s}
, hence
\mathit{c}^{\prime}\Rrightarrow_{s}\infty\in S
, and, for all premises of shape
\mathit{c}^{\prime}\Rrightarrow_{s}\textsc{r}
, with
\textsc{r}\neq\infty
,
\vdash\mathit{c}^{\prime}\Rrightarrow_{s}\textsc{r}
, hence
\mathit{c}^{\prime}\Rrightarrow_{s}\textsc{r}\in S
as well.

•

For each
\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho\in S
, we have
\vdash\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho
, hence this judgment is the consequence of a rule in
Fig.

18
where for each premise, necessarily of shape
\mathit{c}^{\prime}\Rrightarrow_{r^{\prime}}\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\mid\rho^{\prime}
, we have
\vdash\mathit{c}^{\prime}\Rrightarrow_{r^{\prime}}\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\mid\rho^{\prime}
, hence
\mathit{c}^{\prime}\Rrightarrow_{r^{\prime}}\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\mid\rho^{\prime}\in S
.

2.

S
is bounded, that is, each
\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\textsc{r}
in
S
can be inductively derived (has a finite proof tree) using the rules and the corule in
Fig.

22
. This is trivial, since, for
\textsc{r}=\infty
, the judgment can be directly derived by
(co-div)
, and, for
\textsc{r}\neq\infty
, since
\vdash\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\textsc{r}
, this holds by definition.

∎

Thanks to the theorem above, to prove type soundness (formulated as in
Theorem

7.8
) it is enough to prove the progress-
\infty
property for well-typed configurations which do not converge.

Set
\mathit{WT}_{r}=\{\mathit{c}\mid{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{c}:{\tau}^{r}}\ \mbox{for some}\ \Gamma,\tau\}
, and, accordingly with the notation in
Theorem

7.9
,
\mathit{WT}^{\infty}_{r}=\{\mathit{c}\mid\mathit{c}\in\mathit{WT}^{r}\ \mbox{and}\ \not\exists\ \mathcal{V}\mid\rho\ \mbox{such that}\ \vdash_{\infty}\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho\}
.

Theorem 7.10

(Progress-
\infty
)
.

If
\mathit{c}\in\mathit{WT}^{\infty}_{r}
, then
\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\infty
is the consequence of a rule where, for all premises of shape
\mathit{c}^{\prime}\Rrightarrow_{s}\infty
,
\mathit{c}^{\prime}\in\mathit{WT}^{\infty}_{s}
, and, for all premises of shape
\mathit{c}^{\prime}\Rrightarrow_{s}\textsc{r}
, with
\textsc{r}\neq\infty
,
\vdash\mathit{c}^{\prime}\Rrightarrow_{s}\textsc{r}
.

We derive this theorem from the next one, which needs the following notations:

•

We use the metavariable
\tilde{\rho}
for environments where grades have been erased, hence they are maps from variables into values.

•

We write
\mathsf{erase}(\rho)
for the environment obtained from
\rho
by erasing grades and grade contexts.

•

The reduction relation
\Rrightarrow
over pairs
\mathit{v}\mid\tilde{\rho}
and
\mathit{e}\mid\tilde{\rho}
is defined by the metarules in
Fig.

18
where we remove side conditions involving grades and grade contexts . That is, such relation models standard semantics.

The following theorem states that, if a configuration is well-typed, and (ignoring the grades) reduces to a result, then there is a corresponding graded reduction, leading to a result which is well-typed as well.

Theorem 7.11
.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}\mid\rho:{\tau}^{r}
and, set
\tilde{\rho}=\mathsf{erase}(\rho)
,

\mathit{e}\mid\tilde{\rho}\Rrightarrow\mathbf{v}\mid\tilde{\rho}^{\prime}
, then there exist
\rho^{\prime}
,
\gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
such that

\mathit{e}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}^{\gamma}\mid\rho^{\prime}
with
\mathsf{erase}(\rho^{\prime})=\tilde{\rho}^{\prime}
, and

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathbf{v}^{\gamma}\mid\rho^{\prime}:{\tau}^{r}

consistent with
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}\mid\rho:{\tau}^{r}
.

8
Related work

As mentioned, the contributions closest to this work, since they present a resource-aware semantics, are
(
ChoudhuryEEW21
;
BianchiniDGZ@ECOOP23
;
TorczonSAAVW23
)
, and the conference version of this paper
(
BianchiniDGZ@OOPSLA23
)
.
In
(
ChoudhuryEEW21
)
, the authors develop
GraD
, a graded dependent type system that includes functions,
tensor products, additive sums, and a unit type. The instrumented semantics is defined on typed terms, with the only aim to show the role of the type system. In
(
BianchiniDGZ@ECOOP23
)
, where the language is Java-like, the semantics, small-step as well, is given
independently
, to provide a simple execution model taking into account usage of resources. In
(
BianchiniDGZ@OOPSLA23
)
, language and type system are the same of the current paper, and the reduction is big-step, making it no longer necessary to annotate subterms; however, differently from the current paper, such reduction allows waste.
In
(
TorczonSAAVW23
)
reduction is big-step as well, and values are closures that “save” the
environment in the case of delayed evaluation, as in our annotated values. Here, resource annotations can only
count down, and provide an upper bound on those required during computation.

The type system in this paper follows the same design of those in
(
BianchiniDGZ22
;
BianchiniDGZ@TCS23
)
, which, however, consider a Java-like underlying calculus. Such works also deal with two interesting issues not considered here. The former is the definition of a canonical construction leading to a unique grade algebra of
heterogeneous
grades from a family of grade algebras, thus allowing different notions of resource usage to coexist in the same program.
The latter is providing linguistic support
to specify
user-defined
grades, for instance grade annotations could be written themselves in Java, analogously to what
happens with exceptions.

Coming more in general to resource-aware type systems,
coeffects
, that is, grades used as annotations in contexts, were first introduced by
(
PetricekOM13
)
and further analyzed by
(
PetricekOM14
)
.
In particular,
(
PetricekOM14
)
develops a generic coeffect system which augments the simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus with context annotations indexed by
coeffect shapes
.
The proposed framework is very abstract, and the authors focus only on two opposite instances:
structural (per-variable) and flat (whole context) coeffects, identified by specific choices of context shapes.

Most of the subsequent literature on coeffects focuses on structural ones, for which there is a clear algebraic description in terms of semirings.
This was first noticed by
(
BrunelGMZ14
)
, who developed a framework for structural coeffects
for a functional language.
This approach is inspired by a generalization of the exponential modality of linear logic, see, e.g.,
(
BreuvartP15
)
.
That is, the distinction between linear and unrestricted variables of linear systems is generalized to have variables
decorated by coeffects (grades), that determine how much they can be used.
In this setting, many advances have been made to combine grades with other programming features, such as
computational effects
(
GaboardiKOBU16
;
OrchardLE19
;
DalLagoG22
)
,
dependent types
(
Atkey18
;
ChoudhuryEEW21
;
McBride16
)
, and
polymorphism
(
AbelB20
)
.
In all these papers, tracking usage through grades has practical benefits like erasure
of irrelevant terms and compiler optimizations.

McBride16
;
WoodA22
observed that contexts in a structural coeffect system form a module over the semiring of grades, event though they
restrict themselves to free modules, that is, to structural coeffect systems. Recently,
(
BianchiniDGZS22
)
shows a significant non-structural instance, namely, a coeffect system to track sharing in the imperative paradigm.

9
Conclusion

We illustrated, on a paradigmatic calculus, how to enrich semantics and type system of a language, parametrically on an arbitrary grade algebra, so to express and prove resource-aware soundness including no-waste.
That is, for a well-typed program there is a computation which is non-stuck, since, besides type errors, both resource exhaustion and waste are prevented.

To prove such property, we defined two instrumented semantics, the latter being a refinement of the former. In both formulations, the resources consumed by a computation are explicitly tracked, and programs cannot consume more resources than those available. In the refined formulation, in addition, programs cannot waste resources.

The proof of resource-aware soundness provides a significant, complex application of a schema previously introduced in
(
AnconaDZ17
)
, in a case where the semantics is non-deterministic, hence
soundness-may
needs to be proved.

Most works on graded type systems introduce box/unbox operators in the syntax. Hence, programs typed with the type system proposed in this paper could not even be
written
in such systems. A formal comparison with a calculus/type system based on boxing/unboxing is a challenging topic for future work. However, it is not obvious how to make such a comparison, since works which present calculi with a similar expressive power to ours, e.g.,
(
BrunelGMZ14
;
DalLagoG22
)
, do not include an instrumented semantics, so we should as first step develop such a semantics.

Another direction we would like to develop is an extension of the approach to the
imperative
case, so to characterize as grades some properties which are of paramount importance in this context, such as
mutability/immutability
or
uniqueness
opposed to
linearity
, revisiting what discussed in
(
MarshallVO22
)
. Note that, in the imperative case, a different notion of resource usage could be considered, e.g., rather than any time a variable occurrence needs to be replaced, any time memory needs to be accessed, even possibily distinguishing read from write accesses.

Finally, some more general topics to be investigated are type inference, for which the challenging feature are recursive functions, and combination with
effects
, as in
(
DalLagoG22
)
.

Concerning implementation, and mechanization of proofs, which of course would be beneficial developments as well, we just mention the Agda library at
https://github.com/LcicC/inference-systems-agda
, described in
(
CicconeDZ21
)
, which allows one to specify (generalized) inference systems. One of the examples provided in
(
CicconeDZ21
)
is, as in this paper, a big-step semantics including divergence.

Proofs of
Section

of

6.4
.

Item

1
.
We have that
\mathsf{G}(\mathit{p})=\prod_{i\in 1..n}\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x}_{i},\mathit{x}_{i+1})
.
Then, we derive
\mathsf{G}(\mathit{p})=\mathbf{0}
, because

\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x}_{j-1},\mathit{x}_{j})
is equal to
\mathbf{0}
.

Item

2
.
Let
\mathit{p}\in\mathsf{P}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})
, then, because
\mathit{x}\neq\mathit{y}
and
\mathit{y}\notin V(\mathsf{G})
, by
Item

1
, we have
\mathsf{G}(\mathit{p})=\mathbf{0}
.
Hence, this proves that
\mathit{p}\notin\mathsf{P}_{{\neq}\mathbf{0}}^{\mathsf{G}}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})
, as needed.

Item

3
.
Let
\mathit{p}=\mathit{x}_{1}\cdots\mathit{x}_{k}\in\mathsf{P}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})
be a path with
k\geq n+2
.
Then we have

\mathsf{G}(\mathit{p})=\prod_{i\in 1..k-1}\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x}_{i},\mathit{x}_{i+1})
.
We distinguish two cases.
If there is
j\in 2..k
such that
\mathit{x}_{j}\notin V(\mathsf{G})
, then the thesis follows by
Item

1
.
Otherwise, we have that
\mathit{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{k}\in V(\mathsf{G})
, but, since
k\geq n+2
and

has
n
elements, there must be a repeated variable, i.e.,
there are indices

such that
\mathit{x}_{j}=\mathit{x}_{h}
.
Because
\mathsf{G}
is acyclic, we deduce that
\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x}_{j}\cdots\mathit{x}_{h})=\prod_{i\in j..h-1}\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x}_{i},\mathit{x}_{i+1})=\mathbf{0}
and this implies
\mathsf{G}(\mathit{p})=\mathbf{0}
, as needed.

Item

4
.
It is an immediate consequence of
Item

3
.
∎

of
Lemma

6.6
.

The fact that
(\mathsf{G}{\cdot}{\mathsf{G}}^{\star})(\mathit{x},\mathit{x})=(\mathsf{G}_{\mathit{x}}{\cdot}{\mathsf{G}}^{\star})(\mathit{x})
is immediate,
hence we only prove that
(\mathsf{G}_{\mathit{x}}{\cdot}{\mathsf{G}}^{\star})(\mathit{x})=\mathbf{0}
.
By definition, we have

(\mathsf{G}_{\mathit{x}}{\cdot}{\mathsf{G}}^{\star})(\mathit{x})=\sum_{\mathit{y}\in\mathsf{V}}\mathsf{G}_{\mathit{x}}(\mathit{y}){\cdot}{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}(\mathit{y},\mathit{x})=\sum_{\mathit{y}\in\mathsf{V}}\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y}){\cdot}{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}(\mathit{y},\mathit{x})

To conclude the proof, it suffices to show that,
for each
\mathit{y}\in\mathsf{V}
,
\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y}){\cdot}{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}(\mathit{y},\mathit{x})=\mathbf{0}
.
Indeed, we have

\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y}){\cdot}{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}(\mathit{y},\mathit{x})=\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y}){\cdot}\sum_{\mathit{p}\in\mathsf{P}(\mathit{y},\mathit{x})}\mathsf{G}(\mathit{p})=\sum_{\mathit{p}\in\mathsf{P}(\mathit{y},\mathit{x})}\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y}){\cdot}\mathsf{G}(\mathit{p})=\sum_{\mathit{p}\in\mathsf{P}(\mathit{y},\mathit{x})}\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x}\mathit{p})

because
\mathsf{in}(\mathit{p})=\mathit{y}
.
Then, since
\mathsf{out}(\mathit{p})=\mathit{x}
, the path
\mathit{x}\mathit{p}
is a cycle and so
\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x}\mathit{p})=\mathbf{0}
, because
\mathsf{G}
is acyclic.
Therefore, we conclude
\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y}){\cdot}{\mathsf{G}}^{\star}(\mathit{y},\mathit{x})=\mathbf{0}
, as needed.
∎

of
Proposition

6.7
.

We proceed by induction on
n
.
If
n=0
, then
\mathsf{G}^{0}=\mathbb{I}
and, since the only paths of length

\mathit{x}
, the thesis is immediate.
Let

.
Using the induction hypothesis, we derive

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}^{k+1}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{G}{\cdot}\mathsf{G}^{k})(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})=\sum_{z\in\mathsf{V}}\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x},z){\cdot}\mathsf{G}^{k}(z,\mathit{y})

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\mathsf{V}}\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x},z){\cdot}\sum_{\mathit{p}\in\mathsf{P}_{k}(z,\mathit{y})}\mathsf{G}(\mathit{p})=\sum_{z\in\mathsf{V}}\sum_{\mathit{p}\in\mathsf{P}_{k}(z,\mathit{y})}\mathsf{G}(\mathit{x}\cdot\mathit{p})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathit{p}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}_{k+1}(\mathit{x},\mathit{y})}\mathsf{G}(\mathit{p}^{\prime})

where the last step relies on the fact that

and
\mathit{p}\in\mathsf{P}_{k}(z,\mathit{y})
for some

.

For the proof of
Theorem

6.14
, we introduce the following notations:
\gamma{\subseteq}\rho
if
\gamma(\mathit{x})=\mathbf{0}
for each
\mathit{x}\not\in\mathsf{dom}(\rho)
, that is, the support of
\gamma
is a subset of the domain of

\gamma{\|}\rho
if
\gamma^{a}(\mathit{x})=\mathbf{0}
for all
\mathit{x}\in\mathsf{dom}(\rho)
, that is, the support of
\gamma
and the domain of
\rho
are disjoint.

Lemma 10.1
.

If
\gamma{\subseteq}\rho
then
\gamma{\cdot}\mathsf{G}_{\rho+\rho^{\prime}}=\gamma{\cdot}\mathsf{G}_{\rho}
.

Proof.

Consider
\mathit{x}\in\mathsf{dom}(\rho^{\prime})
. If
\mathit{x}\in\mathsf{dom}(\rho)
, since
\rho\|\rho^{\prime}
, we have
\rho(\mathit{x})=\_:\mathcal{V}
and
\rho^{\prime}(\mathit{x})=\_:\mathcal{V}
, hence
\gamma{\cdot}\mathsf{G}_{\rho+\rho^{\prime}}(\mathit{x})=\gamma{\cdot}\mathsf{G}_{\rho}(\mathit{x})
. If
\mathit{x}\not\in\mathsf{dom}(\rho)
we have
\gamma{\cdot}\mathsf{G}_{\rho+\rho^{\prime}}(\mathit{x})=\mathbf{0}
. Hence we get the thesis.
∎

of

.

We prove the following generalized statement:

If
\mathit{E}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}^{\gamma^{\prime}}\mid\rho^{\prime}

3.

\gamma_{\rho^{\prime}}+\gamma^{c}\preceq\gamma_{\rho}+\gamma^{a}

\gamma{\cdot}\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho}+\gamma^{a}\preceq r^{\prime}{\cdot}\gamma^{\prime}{\cdot}\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho^{\prime}}+\gamma^{c}

First, we show that

\gamma_{\rho}+\gamma^{a}

Hence,
\gamma{\cdot}\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho}+\gamma^{a}\preceq\gamma_{\rho}+\gamma^{a}

.

Then, we prove

(var)

\mathit{x}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho^{\prime}

(2)
r+s^{\prime}\preceq s

(3)
r{\cdot}\gamma^{\prime}{\cdot}{\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho^{\prime}}}\preceq\gamma_{\rho^{\prime}}

, hence

\mathit{x}

Item

1

We have
r^{\prime}{\cdot}\gamma^{\prime}{\cdot}\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho^{\prime}}\preceq r{\cdot}\gamma^{\prime}{\cdot}\mathsf{G}^{\star}_{\rho^{\prime}}\preceq\gamma_{\rho^{\prime}}
from (3).

Item

3

We have

\gamma_{\rho^{\prime}}+(\mathit{x}:r^{\prime})=\gamma_{\hat{\rho}},\mathit{x}:s^{\prime}+(\mathit{x}:r^{\prime})=

\gamma_{\hat{\rho}},(\mathit{x}:r^{\prime}+s^{\prime})\preceq\gamma_{\hat{\rho}},(\mathit{x}:r+s^{\prime})\preceq
from (2)

\gamma_{\hat{\rho}},(\mathit{x}:s)=\gamma_{\rho}

Noting that
{(\mathit{x}:r^{\prime}){\cdot}\mathsf{G}_{\rho}=r^{\prime}{\cdot}\gamma^{\prime}}

.

(pair)

We have

(1)
\mathit{v}_{1}\mid\rho_{1}\Rrightarrow_{r{\cdot}r_{1}}\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\gamma^{\prime}_{1}}\mid\rho_{1}^{\prime}

of
Proposition

6.17
.

If
\gamma\vdash\rho\triangleright\Delta
then
\gamma_{\Delta}+\gamma_{\rho}{\cdot}\mathsf{G}_{\rho}\preceq\gamma_{\rho}
.

Set
\rho=\mathit{x}_{1}:\langle{r_{1}},{\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}}\rangle,\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}:\langle{r_{n}},{\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\gamma_{n}}}\rangle
.
To derive
\gamma\vdash\rho\triangleright\Delta
we have
necessarily applied rule
(t-env)
, and then
(t-val)
to the premises, hence

\gamma_{\gamma}=\mathit{x}_{1}:r_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}:r_{n}
, and, for some
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}
, we have
\gamma_{i}\preceq\gamma_{\gamma_{i}}
for
{i\in 1..n}
, and
\Delta+\sum_{i\in 1..n}r_{i}{\cdot}\gamma_{i}\preceq\gamma
.
From the last condition,
\gamma_{\Delta}+\sum_{i\in 1..n}r_{i}{\cdot}\gamma_{\gamma_{i}}\preceq\gamma_{\gamma}
, hence, since
\gamma_{\gamma}=\gamma_{\rho}
, we get
\gamma_{\Delta}+\sum_{i\in 1..n}r_{i}{\cdot}\gamma_{i}\preceq\gamma_{\rho}
.
Finally, we show that
\sum_{i\in 1..n}r_{i}{\cdot}\gamma_{i}=\gamma_{\rho}{\cdot}\mathsf{G}_{\rho}
. Indeed, for
i\in 1..n
,
r_{i}=\gamma_{\rho}(\mathit{x}_{i})
, and
\gamma_{i}(\mathit{x})=\mathsf{G}_{\rho}(\mathit{x}_{i},\mathit{x})
, hence
we have
(\sum_{i\in 1..n}r_{i}{\cdot}\gamma_{i})(\mathit{x})=\sum_{i\in 1..n}\gamma_{\rho}(\mathit{x}_{i}){\cdot}\mathsf{G}_{\rho}(\mathit{x}_{i},\mathit{x})
, which is equal to
(\gamma_{\rho}{\cdot}\mathsf{G}_{\rho})(\mathit{x})=\sum_{\mathit{y}\in\mathsf{V}}\gamma_{\rho}(\mathit{y})\cdot\mathsf{G}_{\rho}(\mathit{y},\mathit{x})
since
\gamma_{\rho}(\mathit{y})\neq\mathbf{0}
implies
\mathit{y}=\mathit{x}_{i}
for some
i\in 1..n
.
∎

Lemma 11.1

(Inversion for value expressions)
.

1.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{x}:{\tau}^{r}
then
\Gamma=\mathit{x}:_{r^{\prime}}\tau,\Gamma^{\prime}
and

\mathit{x}:_{r^{\prime}}\tau\preceq\Gamma
and
r\preceq r^{\prime}
.

2.

If
\Gamma\vdash\texttt{rec}\,\mathit{f}.\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathit{e}:{\tau}^{r}
then
r^{\prime}{\cdot}\Gamma^{\prime}\preceq\Gamma
and
\tau=\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}\rightarrow_{s}\tau_{2}^{r_{2}}
such that
{\Gamma^{\prime},\mathit{f}:_{s}\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}\rightarrow_{s}\tau_{2}^{r_{2}},\mathit{x}:_{r_{1}}\tau_{1}\vdash\mathit{e}:\tau_{2}^{r_{2}}}
and
r\preceq r^{\prime}
.

3.

If
\Gamma\vdash\texttt{unit}:{\tau}^{r}
then
\tau=\texttt{Unit}
and
\emptyset\preceq\Gamma
.

4.

If
\Gamma\vdash\langle^{r_{1}}\mathit{v}_{1},\mathit{v}_{2}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.5968pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{r_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.92628pt{r_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}:{\tau}^{r}
then
r^{\prime}{\cdot}(\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2})\preceq\Gamma
,
\tau=\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}\otimes\tau_{2}^{r_{2}}
and
r\preceq r^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma_{1}\vdash\mathit{v}_{1}:\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}
,
\Gamma_{2}\vdash\mathit{v}_{2}:{\tau_{2}}^{r_{2}}
.

5.

If
\Gamma\vdash\texttt{inl}^{r_{1}}\mathit{v}:{\tau}^{r}
then
r^{\prime}{\cdot}\Gamma^{\prime}\preceq\Gamma
,
\tau=\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}+\tau_{2}^{r_{2}}
and
r\preceq r^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathit{v}:\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}
.

6.

If
\Gamma\vdash\texttt{inr}^{r_{2}}\mathit{v}:{\tau}^{r}
then
r^{\prime}{\cdot}\Gamma^{\prime}\preceq\Gamma
,
\tau=\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}+\tau_{2}^{r_{2}}
and
r\preceq r^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathit{v}:\tau_{2}^{r_{2}}
.

s_{i}=r_{i}{\cdot}r_{i,k}
and

\rho^{\prime}=\mathit{x}_{1}:\langle{r_{1}},{\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}}\rangle,\ldots,\mathit{x}_{k}:\langle{s^{\prime}},{\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\gamma_{k}}}\rangle,\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}:\langle{r_{n}},{\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\gamma_{n}}}\rangle
.

We want to prove that
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathbf{v}^{\gamma_{k}}\mid\rho^{\prime}:{\tau_{k}}^{r}

where
\Gamma^{\prime}
is
\Gamma
in which
r_{k}
is substituted by
s^{\prime}
.
Let
\Delta^{\prime\prime}=r{\cdot}\Gamma_{k}
, from

\Gamma_{k}\vdash\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\gamma_{k}}:{\tau}^{\mathbf{1}}
we get
\Delta^{\prime\prime}\vdash\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\gamma_{k}}:{\tau}^{r}
.
Let
\Gamma_{0}=\sum_{i\in 1..n\ \wedge\ i\neq k}r_{i}{\cdot}\Gamma_{i}
.
From
\sum_{i\in 1..n}r_{i}{\cdot}\Gamma_{i}+\Delta=\Gamma^{\prime\prime}+s{\cdot}\Gamma_{k}+\Delta\preceq\Gamma
we have that
r+s^{\prime}\preceq s
.
Therefore
\Delta^{\prime\prime}+s^{\prime}{\cdot}\Gamma_{k}=(r+s^{\prime}){\cdot}\Gamma_{k}\preceq s{\cdot}\Gamma_{k}
and

\Delta^{\prime\prime}+\Gamma^{\prime\prime}+s^{\prime}{\cdot}\Gamma_{k}\preceq\sum_{i\in 1..n}r_{i}{\cdot}\Gamma_{i}+\Delta
. Since
\Gamma(\mathit{x}_{i})=\Gamma^{\prime}(\mathit{x}_{i})
for all
i\in 1..n
and
i\neq k

and
(\Delta^{\prime\prime}+\Gamma^{\prime\prime}+s^{\prime}{\cdot}\Gamma_{k})(\mathit{x})=s^{\prime}
we have that
\Delta^{\prime\prime}+\Gamma^{\prime\prime}+s^{\prime}{\cdot}\Gamma_{k}\preceq\Gamma^{\prime}
. Applying rule

(t-res)
we get
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathbf{v}^{\gamma_{k}}\mid\rho^{\prime}:{\tau_{k}}^{r}
. Finally from
Theorem

6.20
(
2
) we get

r{\cdot}\gamma_{k}\preceq^{\star}_{{\tiny\mathsf{nw}}}\rho^{\prime}
, so rule
(var)
can be applied and
\mathit{x}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathbf{v}^{\gamma_{k}}\mid\rho^{\prime}
.

For function and unit values the result derives from
Theorem

6.20
(
2
).

\mathit{v}=\langle^{s_{1}}\mathit{v}_{1},\mathit{v}_{2}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{s_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.54688pt{s_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{s_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.54688pt{s_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{s_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.89064pt{s_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{s_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.89064pt{s_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}

From
\Delta\vdash\langle^{s_{1}}\mathit{v}_{1},\mathit{v}_{2}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{s_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.54688pt{s_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{s_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.54688pt{s_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{s_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.89064pt{s_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{s_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.89064pt{s_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}:{\tau}^{r}
for some
\Delta
.
By
Lemma

11.1
(
4
)
for some
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
and
r^{\prime}
we get
r^{\prime}{\cdot}(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2})\preceq\Delta
,
\tau=\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}\otimes\tau_{2}^{s_{2}}

and
r\preceq r^{\prime}
and
\Delta_{i}\vdash\mathit{v}_{i}:\tau_{i}^{s_{i}}
for
i=1,2
.
From
r^{\prime}{\cdot}(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2})\preceq\Delta
and
r\preceq r^{\prime}
we get
(r{\cdot}\Delta_{1})+(r{\cdot}\Delta_{2})\preceq\Delta

and from
\Gamma\vdash\rho\triangleright\Delta
and
Lemma

11.6
we have that
there are
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
such that

\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}\preceq\rho
and

\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}\vdash\rho_{1}\triangleright r{\cdot}\Delta_{1}
and

\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}\vdash\rho_{2}\triangleright r{\cdot}\Delta_{2}
and these are consistent.
Therefore from
r{\cdot}\Delta_{i}\vdash\mathit{v}_{i}:\tau_{i}^{r{\cdot}s_{i}}
and

\Gamma^{\prime}_{i}\vdash\rho_{i}\triangleright r{\cdot}\Delta_{i}
, applying rule
(T-conf)
we get
\Gamma^{\prime}_{i}\vdash\mathit{v}_{i}\mid\rho_{i}:{\tau}^{\tau_{i}^{r{\cdot}s_{i}}}
.
By inductive hypotheses

\mathit{v}_{i}\mid\rho_{i}\Rrightarrow_{r{\cdot}s_{i}}\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{i}}\mid\rho_{i}^{\prime}
and therefore

\langle^{s_{1}}\mathit{v}_{1},\mathit{v}_{2}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{s_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.54688pt{s_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{s_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.54688pt{s_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{s_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.89064pt{s_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{s_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.89064pt{s_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}\mid\rho\Rrightarrow_{r}\langle^{s_{1}}\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{s_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.54688pt{s_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{s_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-8.54688pt{s_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{s_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.89064pt{s_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{s_{2}}}}}\rangle^{{\kern-7.89064pt{s_{2}}\kern 3.8889pt}}_{{\kern-3.50002pt\kern 3.8889pt}}}^{\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{1}+\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{2}}\mid(\rho_{1}^{\prime}+\rho_{2}^{\prime})
.

∎

Lemma 11.7

(Inversion for possibly diverging expressions)
.

1.

If
\Gamma\vdash\texttt{return}\ \mathit{v}:{\tau}^{r}
then
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathit{v}:{\tau}^{r^{\prime}}
with
r\preceq r^{\prime}
,
\Gamma^{\prime}\preceq\Gamma
and
r^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{0}
.

2.

If
\Gamma\vdash\texttt{let}\ \mathit{x}=\mathit{e}_{1}\ \texttt{in}\ \mathit{e}_{2}:{\tau}^{r}
then
\Gamma_{1}\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}:\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}
and
\Gamma_{2},\mathit{x}:_{r_{1}}\tau_{1}\vdash\mathit{e}_{2}:{\tau}^{r^{\prime}}
and
\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\preceq\Gamma
and
r\preceq r^{\prime}
.

3.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{v}_{1}\,{\!\mathit{v}_{2}}:{\tau_{2}}^{r}
then we have
\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\preceq\Gamma
and
r\preceq r^{\prime}{\cdot}r_{2}
such that
\Gamma_{1}\vdash\mathit{v}_{1}:{(\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}\rightarrow_{s}\tau_{2}^{r_{2}})}^{r^{\prime}+r^{\prime}{\cdot}s}
and
\Gamma_{2}\vdash\mathit{v}_{2}:{\tau_{1}}^{r^{\prime}{\cdot}r_{1}}
and
r^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{0}
.

4.

If
\Gamma\vdash\texttt{match}\ \mathit{v}\ \texttt{with}\ \texttt{unit}\ \rightarrow\mathit{e}:{\tau}^{r}
then we have
\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\preceq\Gamma
and
r\preceq t
such that
\Gamma_{1}\vdash\mathit{v}:{\texttt{Unit}}^{r^{\prime}}
and
\Gamma_{2}\vdash\mathit{e}:{\tau}^{t}
.

5.

If
\Gamma\vdash\texttt{match}\ \mathit{v}\ \texttt{with}\ \langle\mathit{x},\mathit{y}\rangle\rightarrow\mathit{e}:{\tau}^{r}
then we have
\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\preceq\Gamma
and
r\preceq t
such that
\Gamma_{1}\vdash\mathit{v}:{({\tau_{1}}^{r_{1}}\otimes{\tau_{2}}^{r_{2}})}^{s}
and
\Gamma_{2},\mathit{x}:_{s{\cdot}r_{1}}\tau,\mathit{y}:_{s{\cdot}r_{2}}\tau_{2}\vdash\mathit{e}:{\tau}^{t}
and
s\neq\mathbf{0}
.

6.

If
\Gamma\vdash\texttt{match}\ \mathit{v}\ \texttt{with}\ \texttt{inl}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathit{e}_{1}\ \texttt{or}\ \texttt{inr}\,\mathit{x}\rightarrow\mathit{e}_{2}:{\tau}^{r}
then we have
\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\preceq\Gamma
and
r\preceq s
such that
\Gamma_{1}\vdash\mathit{v}:{({\tau_{1}}^{r_{1}}+{\tau_{2}}^{r_{2}})}^{r^{\prime}}
,
\Gamma_{2},\mathit{x}:_{r^{\prime}{\cdot}r_{1}}\tau_{1}\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}:{\tau}^{s}
,
\Gamma_{2},\mathit{x}:_{r^{\prime}{\cdot}r_{2}}\tau_{2}\vdash\mathit{e}_{2}:{\tau}^{s}
and
r^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{0}
.

Lemma 11.8

(Environment extension)
.

If
\Gamma_{1}\vdash\rho_{1}\triangleright\Delta_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}\vdash\mathcal{V}\mid\rho_{2}:{\tau}^{r}
and these are consistent, then

\rho=\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}
and
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}
, then

\Gamma,\mathit{x}:_{r}\tau\vdash\rho,\mathit{x}:\langle{r},{\mathcal{V}}\rangle\triangleright\Delta_{1},\mathit{x}:_{r}\tau
.

Proof.

By consistency, we know that

\rho=\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}
and
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}
are well defined.
Let

\rho=\mathit{x}_{1}:\langle{r_{1}},{\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}}\rangle,\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}:\langle{r_{n}},{\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\gamma_{n}}}\rangle
and

\Gamma=\mathit{x}_{1}:_{r_{1}}\tau_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}:_{r_{n}}\tau_{n}
and suppose, without loss of generality, that
there are
\leq h\leq k\leq n+1
such that

\mathsf{dom}(\rho_{1})\cap\mathsf{dom}(\rho_{2})=\{\mathit{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{h-1}\}
,
\mathsf{dom}(\rho_{1})\setminus\mathsf{dom}(\rho_{2})=\{\mathit{x}_{h},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{k-1}\}
and
\mathsf{dom}(\rho_{2})\setminus\mathsf{dom}(\rho_{1})=\{\mathit{x}_{k},\ldots,\mathit{x}_{n}\}
.
Hence, for all
i\in 1..h-1
, we have

r_{i}=r^{\prime}_{i}+r^{\prime\prime}_{i}
where

\Gamma_{1}(\mathit{x}_{i})=\langle{r^{\prime}_{i}},{\tau_{i}}\rangle
and
\Gamma_{2}(\mathit{x}_{i})=\langle{r^{\prime\prime}_{i}},{\tau_{i}}\rangle
and similarly for the environments.
By Rule
(t-conf)
, we know that

\Gamma_{2}\vdash\rho_{2}\triangleright\Delta_{2}
and
\Delta_{2}\vdash\mathcal{V}:{\tau}^{r}
.
By consistency and Rules
(t-env)
and
(t-val)
, we deduce that,
for all
i\in 1..n
,
\Theta_{i}\vdash\mathbf{v}_{i}:{r_{i}}^{\tau_{i}}
and
the following inequalities hold:

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{h-1}r^{\prime}_{i}{\cdot}\Theta_{i}+\sum_{i=h}^{k-1}r_{i}{\cdot}\Theta_{i}

\displaystyle\preceq\Gamma_{1}

\displaystyle\Delta_{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{h-1}r^{\prime\prime}_{i}{\cdot}\Theta_{i}+\sum_{i=k}^{n}r_{i}{\cdot}\Theta_{i}

\displaystyle\preceq\Gamma_{2}

Combining these inequalities, we derive

\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}{\cdot}\Theta_{i}\preceq\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma

Therefore, since
\mathit{x}\notin\mathsf{dom}(\rho)
, by Rule
(t-env)
we immediately get the thesis.
∎

of
Theorem

7.10
.

We have
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}\mid\rho:{\tau}^{r}
. By rule
(t-conf)
we have
\Gamma\vdash\rho\triangleright\Delta
and
\Delta\vdash\mathit{e}:{\tau}^{r}
.
Proof is by case analysis on
\mathit{e}
. We show only one case for the sake of brevity, since other ones are similar.

\mathit{e}=\texttt{let}\ \mathit{x}=\mathit{e}_{1}\ \texttt{in}\ \mathit{e}_{2}

By
Lemma

11.7
(
2
) we have
\Delta_{1}\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}:\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}
and
\Delta_{2},\mathit{x}:_{r_{1}}\tau_{1}\vdash\mathit{e}_{2}:{\tau}^{r^{\prime}}
and
\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}\preceq\Delta
and
r\preceq r^{\prime}
. By
Lemma

11.6
we have
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
such that

\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}\preceq\rho
and

\Gamma_{1}\vdash\rho_{1}\triangleright\Delta_{1}
and

\Gamma_{2}\vdash\rho_{2}\triangleright\Delta_{2}
. By rule
(t-conf)
we have
\Gamma_{1}\vdash\mathit{e}_{1}\mid\rho_{1}:\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}
.
We have two cases:

•

\not\exists

\mathcal{V}\mid\rho_{1}^{\prime}
such that
\mathit{e}_{1}\mid\rho_{1}\Rrightarrow_{r_{1}}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho_{1}^{\prime}

By applying rule
(let-div1)
we have the thesis.

•

\exists

\mathcal{V}\mid\rho_{1}^{\prime}
such that
\mathit{e}_{1}\mid\rho_{1}\Rrightarrow_{r_{1}}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho_{1}^{\prime}

Since we have
\mathit{e}_{1}\mid\rho_{1}\Rrightarrow_{r_{1}}\mathcal{V}\mid\rho_{1}^{\prime}
we also have
\mathit{e}_{1}\mid\tilde{\rho}_{1}\Rrightarrow\mathcal{V}\mid\tilde{\rho}^{\prime}_{1}
with
\mathsf{erase}(\rho_{1})=\tilde{\rho}_{1}
and
\mathsf{erase}(\rho_{1}^{\prime})=\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{\prime}
. By
Theorem

7.11
we have
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\vdash\mathcal{V}\mid\rho_{1}^{\prime}:\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}
.
Since
\Gamma_{2}\vdash\rho_{2}\triangleright\Delta_{2}
and
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\vdash\mathcal{V}\mid\rho_{1}^{\prime}:\tau_{1}^{r_{1}}
and
\hat{\rho}=\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}+\Gamma_{2}
, then

\Gamma^{\prime},\mathit{x}:_{r_{1}}\tau_{1}\vdash\hat{\rho},\mathit{x}:\langle{r_{1}},{\mathcal{V}}\rangle\triangleright\Delta_{2},\mathit{x}:_{r_{1}}\tau_{1}
. By renaming we have
\Gamma^{\prime},\mathit{x}^{\prime}:_{r_{1}}\tau_{1}\vdash\hat{\rho},\mathit{x}^{\prime}:\langle{r_{1}},{\mathcal{V}}\rangle\triangleright\Delta_{2},\mathit{x}^{\prime}:_{r_{1}}\tau_{1}
. By
Lemma

11.3
we have
\Delta_{2},\mathit{x}^{\prime}:_{r_{1}}\tau_{1}\vdash\mathit{e}_{2}[\mathit{x}^{\prime}/\mathit{x}]:{\tau}^{r^{\prime}}
. By
(t-conf)
and
(t-sub)
we have
\Gamma^{\prime},\mathit{x}^{\prime}:_{r_{1}}\tau_{1}\vdash\mathit{e}_{2}[\mathit{x}^{\prime}/\mathit{x}]\mid\hat{\rho},\mathit{x}^{\prime}:\langle{r_{1}},{\mathcal{V}}\rangle:{\tau}^{r}
.

If
\not\exists

\mathcal{V}_{2}\mid\rho_{3}
such that
\mathit{e}_{2}[\mathit{x}^{\prime}/\mathit{x}]\mid\hat{\rho},\mathit{x}^{\prime}:\langle{r_{1}},{\mathcal{V}}\rangle\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}_{2}\mid\rho_{3}
then by rule
(let/let-div2)
we have the thesis, otherwise we have an absurd, since if
\mathcal{V}_{2}\mid\rho_{3}
would exists then, by rule
(let/let-div2)
also
\mathit{c}\Rrightarrow_{r}\mathcal{V}_{2}\mid\rho_{3}
.

∎

of
Theorem

7.11
.

By induction on the syntax of
\mathit{e}
. We show the case
\mathit{e}=\texttt{let}\ \mathit{x}=\mathit{e}_{1}\ \texttt{in}\ \mathit{e}_{2}

(t-conf)
, for some
\Delta
, we get
\Delta\vdash\mathit{e}:{\tau}^{r}
and

(

) we get

, there are

\mathit{e}_{1}
, with

and

and
\rho_{1}

we get that,

(\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}+\Gamma_{2}),\mathit{x}^{\prime}:_{s_{1}}\tau_{1}\vdash(\rho^{\prime}_{1}+\rho_{2}),\mathit{x}^{\prime}:\langle{s_{1}},{\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}}\rangle\triangleright\Delta_{2},\mathit{x}^{\prime}:_{s_{1}}\tau_{1}

(\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}+\Gamma_{2}),\mathit{x}^{\prime}:_{s_{1}}\tau_{1}\vdash\mathit{e}^{\prime}_{2}\mid(\rho^{\prime}_{1}+\rho_{2}),\mathit{x}^{\prime}:\langle{s_{1}},{\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}}\rangle:{\tau}^{r}

\mathit{e}^{\prime}_{2}
, (

) with

\mathit{e}^{\prime}_{2}[\mathit{x}^{\prime}/\mathit{x}]\mid(\tilde{\rho}_{1}^{\prime}+\tilde{\rho}_{2}),\mathit{x}:\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{1}\Rrightarrow\mathbf{v}\mid\tilde{\rho}^{\prime}

and
